Table 1
Rationality, Heuristics, Cognitive Biases, and Framing
1. Q: How do rationality and cognitive biases influence critical thinking and everyday judgment?
A: Show answer
Critical thinking requires more than knowing how to identify a weak argument. It also requires understanding how people form beliefs, interpret evidence, make decisions, and respond to persuasive messages. Rationality concerns whether beliefs are supported by evidence and whether actions serve relevant goals. Cognitive biases and heuristics affect which information is noticed, how it is interpreted, and which conclusions feel convincing. Fast intuitive processing is useful in familiar situations, but can mislead when a judgment depends on probability, comparison, evidence quality, or competing explanations. The chapter therefore connects rationality, heuristics and biases, and framing: each helps explain why apparently reasonable people can reach unreliable conclusions and how reflective scrutiny can improve those conclusions.
Study instruction:
- "Map the chapter's three main themes—rationality, heuristics and biases, and framing—and explain how each can affect a real decision."
Key point:
- Critical thinking evaluates both the argument and the psychological processes shaping its reception.
- Bias awareness is a tool for better judgment, not proof that a particular person or conclusion is irrational.
Source links:

- Chong and Druckman: Framing Theory
2. Q: What is cognitive rationality?
A: Show answer
Cognitive rationality concerns the grounds on which a person holds a belief. A belief is cognitively irrational when it is poorly supported by the available evidence, conflicts with other beliefs the person accepts, or is maintained despite strong contrary information without an adequate reason. The chapter's example of refusing to accept evidence of a partner's affair illustrates a belief protected from evidence. The issue is not whether a belief is emotionally understandable, but whether the strength and persistence of the belief are proportionate to the evidence. Cognitive rationality therefore requires attention to consistency, evidential support, alternative explanations, and willingness to revise conclusions when the facts change.
Study instruction:
- "Take one belief you hold strongly and list the evidence for it, evidence against it, and what would cause you to revise it."
Key point:
- Cognitive rationality evaluates belief quality against evidence and logical consistency.
- Confidence should be responsive to the strength, relevance, and completeness of evidence.
3. Q: What is practical or functional rationality?
A: Show answer
Practical rationality concerns whether decisions and actions are aligned with a person's goals and priorities. A choice is practically rational when, given the person's relevant aims and a reasonably accurate view of the situation, it is an appropriate means of pursuing those aims. The chapter contrasts a student who wants to pass an exam but abandons revision for a night out with someone who has finished exams and chooses the same evening out to relax. The outward behaviour can be identical while its rationality differs because the goals differ. Evaluating practical rationality therefore requires identifying the actual objective, ranking competing wants, and considering the likely consequences of available actions.
Study instruction:
- "For a recent decision, identify the goal you were pursuing, competing preferences, and whether the action advanced the priority goal."
Key point:
- Practical rationality assesses goal-directed decisions and actions, not simply whether a belief is true.
- The same action can be rational for one person or situation and irrational for another because goals differ.
4. Q: How do cognitive and practical rationality differ, and why should they be assessed separately?
A: Show answer
Cognitive rationality asks whether beliefs are justified by evidence and consistent with one another. Practical rationality asks whether actions and decisions advance the goals a person actually has. A person may correctly believe that study is necessary to pass an examination yet choose an activity that undermines that goal; the belief is cognitively sound while the action is practically misaligned. Conversely, a mistaken belief about the need for practice may make a decision appear goal-directed, even though the underlying belief is inaccurate. Separating the two tests prevents an evaluator from assuming that a sensible intention guarantees sound factual judgment, or that a correct belief automatically produces effective behaviour.
Study instruction:
- "Analyse a case twice: first test the person's beliefs against evidence, then test their actions against their stated priorities."
Key point:
- A sound belief and a goal-consistent action are different dimensions of rationality.
- Good decisions benefit from both an accurate picture of reality and clear priorities.
Source links:

- Frankish: Dual-Process and Dual-System Theories of Reasoning
5. Q: Why do self-understanding and an accurate view of the world matter for practical rationality?
A: Show answer
A person cannot reliably choose means to an end without understanding both the end and the situation in which the choice will be made. Self-understanding involves recognising one's wants, commitments, and priorities rather than treating every immediate desire as equally important. An accurate view of the world involves knowing what the available options can realistically achieve, what constraints apply, and what risks or trade-offs follow. The chapter notes that practical rationality can be undermined by an inaccurate conviction about the demands of a task. Reflective decision-making should therefore test both sides: 'Is this what I genuinely want to prioritise?' and 'Is my understanding of the likely consequences correct?'
Study instruction:
- "Before a consequential decision, write down your priority objective, assumptions about the situation, and how each assumption can be checked."
Key point:
- Practical rationality requires clarity about goals and accurate beliefs about relevant circumstances.
- A mistaken picture of the world can make a goal-directed choice ineffective.
6. Q: What are System 1 and System 2 thinking?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the familiar dual-process distinction associated with Daniel Kahneman. System 1 describes fast, automatic processing that usually requires little effort or deliberate control. System 2 describes effortful processing that allocates attention to demanding activities such as complex calculation, unfamiliar problems, and careful analysis. Routine driving on an empty road and recognising a simple sentence illustrate automatic processing; handling an unexpected manoeuvre or evaluating a difficult argument requires more deliberate attention. These labels are useful shorthand for different modes of processing, not literal separate organs or a claim that every thought fits neatly into two isolated boxes.
Study instruction:
- "Classify everyday tasks by how much deliberate attention they require, then identify when an automatic response should be checked."
Key point:
- System 1 is generally fast and automatic; System 2 is generally effortful and reflective.
- The model is a practical distinction between modes of processing, not a complete map of the mind.
Source links:

- Frankish: Dual-Process and Dual-System Theories of Reasoning
7. Q: Why is the System 1/System 2 distinction useful even though theories of thinking are more nuanced?
A: Show answer
The chapter acknowledges disagreement about whether human thinking consists of discrete systems or a continuum from automatic to controlled processing. The distinction is still useful because it highlights a practical contrast: routine judgments can be made quickly with little conscious effort, whereas unfamiliar, complex, or high-consequence judgments need more concentrated attention. Treating the model as a heuristic avoids overstating it. It helps learners notice when an intuitive answer is being generated and decide whether the task warrants more analysis. It should not be used to imply that all intuition is wrong, that all deliberate thought is correct, or that the mind operates through two completely independent mechanisms.
Study instruction:
- "Explain both the usefulness and the limitations of dual-process terminology; avoid presenting the two-system metaphor as an undisputed literal architecture."
Key point:
- The distinction helps identify when more deliberate scrutiny is needed.
- The chapter treats the model as a useful simplification while recognising more nuanced accounts.
Source links:

- Frankish: Dual-Process and Dual-System Theories of Reasoning
8. Q: What kinds of tasks are well suited to automatic System 1 processing?
A: Show answer
Automatic processing is well suited to familiar, repeated situations in which the environment is reasonably stable and rapid responses are adequate. Examples include understanding straightforward language, performing simple familiar arithmetic, and routine driving on a clear road. Automation reduces the mental effort needed for everyday activity and leaves attention available for less familiar problems. The danger is not that automatic processing exists, but that a person may apply a familiar shortcut to a situation whose structure has changed or whose stakes require closer inspection. A routine that is safe under normal conditions may become inadequate when unusual signals, conflicting information, or serious consequences arise.
Study instruction:
- "Identify three tasks you perform automatically and describe the conditions under which you would stop and reassess."
Key point:
- Automatic processing is efficient for familiar and predictable tasks.
- A change in context or stakes can make a formerly reliable shortcut unsafe.
Source links:

- Frankish: Dual-Process and Dual-System Theories of Reasoning
9. Q: When should a person deliberately engage System 2 thinking?
A: Show answer
Deliberate processing is especially important when the problem is unfamiliar, complex, consequential, ambiguous, or dependent on probability and evidence that intuition may misread. It is also warranted when an initial answer comes unusually easily despite a demanding question, when important alternatives have not been considered, or when a decision could cause significant harm if wrong. System 2 can compare explanations, calculate, check assumptions, and evaluate an argument's structure. It is not an unlimited resource, however, and effortful thought can still be biased. The aim is to allocate reflective attention where it has the greatest value and use explicit checks rather than equating effort with correctness.
Study instruction:
- "Create a personal pause rule for high-stakes or unfamiliar decisions, including at least one evidence check and one alternative explanation."
Key point:
- Reflective attention is most valuable where complexity, novelty, uncertainty, or consequences are high.
- Slow thinking can reduce some errors but does not make a judgment automatically correct.
Source links:

- Frankish: Dual-Process and Dual-System Theories of Reasoning
10. Q: How does the chapter's discussion of Epstein's experiential and rational systems deepen the two-system account?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents Epstein's experiential system as strongly influenced by concrete experience, images, emotion, pleasure, and the desire for a coherent and positive view of oneself and the world. Its counterpart, the rational system, is more receptive to abstraction, arguments, and evidence. This account helps explain why a person may acknowledge contrary information intellectually yet remain emotionally attached to an established conviction. Beliefs can be sustained not only because of how evidence is assessed, but also because they meet needs for identity, certainty, self-esteem, trust, or comfort. The chapter mainly uses System 1/System 2 language for simplicity while retaining the insight that motivation and emotion shape intuitive processing.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the chapter's System 1/System 2 shorthand with the experiential/rational distinction and identify what the latter adds."
Key point:
- Intuitive belief formation is shaped by emotion and motivation as well as speed.
- A factual correction may not change a conviction if the belief also serves psychological needs.
Source links:

- Frankish: Dual-Process and Dual-System Theories of Reasoning
11. Q: Why can System 1 continue to influence a person who is trying to think carefully?
A: Show answer
Deliberate reasoning does not occur in a psychological vacuum. Automatic associations, salient memories, stereotypes, emotional reactions, and initial interpretations can influence which explanations occur to a person and which evidence seems relevant. System 2 may be concentrating on a problem while System 1 continues to supply suggestions in the background. The chapter therefore warns that even focused thought can be affected by vivid but unrepresentative memories, crude categories, and mild emotional responses. The practical lesson is to build safeguards into deliberation: state assumptions, seek independent information, consider disconfirming evidence, and ask whether an intuitive reaction has quietly set the direction of the analysis.
Study instruction:
- "When reviewing a conclusion, identify the initial impression, emotionally salient facts, and assumptions that may have influenced the later analysis."
Key point:
- Effortful reasoning remains vulnerable to automatic cues and emotional intrusions.
- A structured review is more reliable than simply trying harder to concentrate.
Source links:

- Frankish: Dual-Process and Dual-System Theories of Reasoning
12. Q: What is a heuristic, and why do people rely on heuristics?
A: Show answer
A heuristic is a practical rule of thumb that helps a person make a judgment quickly without analysing every available detail. The chapter describes heuristics as shortcuts that can produce better-than-chance judgments while conserving limited time and mental effort. A person cannot investigate every routine choice as if it were a research project, so automatic judgments are often useful. A heuristic may substitute an easier question for a difficult one—for example, judging how likely something is by how readily an example comes to mind. The same shortcut becomes risky when the easier question is not a good substitute for the question that actually needs answering.
Study instruction:
- "For a familiar shortcut, identify the difficult question, the easier question being substituted, and when that substitution stops being reliable."
Key point:
- Heuristics make everyday judgment efficient under limited time and attention.
- A useful shortcut can still produce error when its cue is poorly matched to the decision.
13. Q: How should a critical thinker distinguish a useful heuristic from a harmful cognitive bias?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses 'bias' in more than one sense. A heuristic is inherently selective: it privileges certain cues because doing so can make decisions manageable. That tendency may be justified by practical benefits in suitable circumstances. A more serious cognitive bias is a predictable distortion or systematic error that leads judgments away from relevant evidence, sound probability, or fair consideration of alternatives. The boundary is not always clear, and terminology in the literature is not perfectly consistent. A critical thinker should therefore ask what information the shortcut uses, what it ignores, whether the ignored information matters, and whether the resulting error occurs predictably in the type of situation being assessed.
Study instruction:
- "Analyse a shortcut by identifying its cue, the omitted information, its benefits, and the predictable situations in which it misleads."
Key point:
- Selectivity is not automatically irrational; its value depends on the task and context.
- A bias becomes problematic when it systematically distorts a judgment or blocks relevant evidence.
14. Q: How can awareness of heuristics improve metacognition and critical thinking?
A: Show answer
Metacognition is awareness of one's own thinking and the ability to monitor and regulate it. Knowing common heuristics helps a person recognise situations in which a quick judgment may be unreliable—for example, when a vivid anecdote is being used to estimate frequency or a familiar stereotype is being used to infer an individual's role. Awareness creates an opportunity to pause, identify the mental shortcut, and choose a more suitable method. Critical thinking can also improve the quality of the shortcuts themselves by teaching more reliable criteria for assessing expertise, relevance, and evidence. The objective is not to eliminate every intuitive judgment, but to make the person more alert to when a shortcut requires correction.
Study instruction:
- "Keep a short decision diary recording the cue you relied on, the alternative evidence, and whether later information changed the result."
Key point:
- Metacognition helps people notice and regulate their own reasoning processes.
- Learning can improve both deliberate analysis and the cues used by quick judgments.
15. Q: What is confirmation bias, and how does it distort the evaluation of evidence?
A: Show answer
Confirmation bias, sometimes called myside bias, is the tendency to seek, notice, interpret, and remember information that supports an existing belief while giving less attention to information that challenges it. The chapter illustrates how a person may acknowledge that data exists but interpret it through a prior theory so strongly that the evidence is no longer represented accurately. Confirmation bias can affect informal reasoning and the reporting or review of research. It changes more than the amount of information someone sees: it can shape how apparently neutral information is understood. A conclusion may therefore feel evidence-based even though the search and interpretation process has been systematically one-sided.
Study instruction:
- "Choose a current belief and deliberately record the strongest available evidence that would count against it, not merely evidence that supports it."
Key point:
- Confirmation bias privileges belief-consistent information during search, attention, interpretation, and memory.
- A fair review must represent contrary evidence accurately rather than dismiss it reflexively.
Source links:

- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias: A Ubiquitous Phenomenon in Many Guises
16. Q: Why is actively seeking disconfirming evidence essential to rational inquiry?
A: Show answer
Evidence that fits a theory may be compatible with that theory without showing that it is the best explanation. A claim becomes more informative when it survives a serious attempt to find evidence that would undermine it. The chapter contrasts casual everyday reasoning with scientific practice: researchers should consider what disconfirmation would look like and seek it, rather than treating supportive observations as sufficient. In practical decisions, the same principle means identifying what result would change a recommendation, what alternative explanation could account for the facts, and what evidence is missing. Disconfirming tests reduce the risk that a weak claim is protected by selective attention or an unfalsifiable interpretation.
Study instruction:
- "Before accepting an important claim, write one specific observation that would weaken it and determine how to look for that observation."
Key point:
- A claim is tested more rigorously when potential counterevidence is actively sought.
- Evidence should be evaluated for what it can disprove as well as what it appears to confirm.
Source links:

- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias: A Ubiquitous Phenomenon in Many Guises
17. Q: How can online echo chambers intensify confirmation bias?
A: Show answer
An echo chamber repeatedly exposes a person to arguments, news, and social feedback that resemble views already held. When users select sources or communities that share their beliefs, compatible messages become more visible and memorable while alternative interpretations may rarely be encountered. The chapter observes that social and news preferences can exaggerate confirmation bias in this way. Repetition can create a misleading sense that a position is widely and independently supported, even when the information comes from a narrow network or repeated versions of the same original claim. The problem is not simply exposure to like-minded people; it is the loss of meaningful independent testing and accurate representation of disagreement.
Study instruction:
- "Trace several posts about one disputed claim back to their original sources and check whether they provide independent evidence or repeat the same claim."
Key point:
- Repeated agreement within a narrow information network is not the same as independent corroboration.
- A broader and more diverse evidence search can reduce the reinforcing effect of echo chambers.
Source links:

- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias: A Ubiquitous Phenomenon in Many Guises
18. Q: How can a systematic review reduce confirmation bias in research?
A: Show answer
A systematic review uses predefined questions and transparent procedures to search for, select, and assess relevant studies. The chapter highlights rules for unbiased selection and evaluation, peer review of the review process, and transparency about how evidence was gathered. These features reduce the opportunity for a researcher to select only favourable findings or let personal expectations determine which studies count. A systematic review does not guarantee truth: the result still depends on the quality of available studies, search coverage, inclusion criteria, and synthesis methods. Its strength is that the method can be inspected and challenged rather than relying on an undocumented selection of supportive evidence.
Study instruction:
- "For a research question, draft inclusion criteria and a search strategy before inspecting results, then record exclusions and reasons."
Key point:
- Transparent selection rules help prevent selective use of favourable studies.
- Systematic methods improve auditability but cannot repair every weakness in the underlying evidence.
Source links:

- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias: A Ubiquitous Phenomenon in Many Guises
19. Q: What is the self-serving bias?
A: Show answer
The self-serving bias is a tendency to interpret events in ways that protect or enhance one's self-image. The chapter describes people taking too much credit for success while assigning too little responsibility to themselves for failure. It can also support optimistic beliefs about personal ability, control, or future outcomes. This tendency is not necessarily a deliberate lie: motivated reasoning can lead a person to notice, remember, or construct explanations that preserve a favourable view of the self. In workplaces, it can distort performance reviews, project retrospectives, and assessments of responsibility unless individuals compare their explanations with independent records and the perspectives of other people involved.
Study instruction:
- "Review a successful outcome and a failed outcome; for each, separate your contribution from external factors using evidence rather than self-protective assumptions."
Key point:
- Self-serving interpretations can distort accountability and learning from both success and failure.
- Balanced review allocates credit and responsibility according to evidence.
20. Q: What is the bias blind spot, and why does it make self-correction difficult?
A: Show answer
The bias blind spot is the tendency to recognise cognitive and motivational biases more readily in other people than in oneself. A person may identify another person's self-serving explanation or selective reasoning while believing their own judgment is objective. This makes ordinary advice to 'watch out for bias' inadequate if the person assumes the warning applies mainly to others. The chapter links this blind spot to motivated reasoning and the difficulty of assessing one's own mental processes from the inside. Practical safeguards should therefore rely on observable procedures—independent review, pre-defined criteria, disclosure of assumptions, and documented alternatives—rather than confidence in one's personal immunity.
Study instruction:
- "When evaluating a disagreement, write down how your own view might be biased before diagnosing the other party's bias."
Key point:
- People often see bias more easily in others than in themselves.
- Independent procedures are more reliable than self-confidence as protection against bias.
Source links:

- Pronin, Lin, and Ross: The Bias Blind Spot
21. Q: What is motivated reasoning, and how can it preserve a preferred worldview?
A: Show answer
Motivated reasoning occurs when the desire to reach or preserve a particular conclusion influences how information is searched, interpreted, and evaluated. The chapter connects this process with the need for a stable worldview, self-esteem, trust, and pleasurable feelings. These needs can encourage people to dismiss opposing ideas unfairly or reinterpret experience to protect an existing position. A person may therefore experience reasoning as sincere while unconsciously applying unequal standards to evidence for and against the preferred conclusion. A useful safeguard is to apply the same evidential test to both sides, make assumptions explicit, and ask an independent reviewer to identify where the reasoning standard changes.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the evidence threshold you apply to a claim you support with the threshold you apply to a claim you oppose."
Key point:
- Motives can influence reasoning without the person being consciously deceptive.
- Equal standards for supportive and opposing evidence help reveal motivated evaluation.
Source links:

- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias: A Ubiquitous Phenomenon in Many Guises
22. Q: What is rationalization, and how does it protect a person from uncomfortable information?
A: Show answer
Rationalization is an explanation offered to make behaviour or an outcome appear acceptable when it does not accurately identify the real reason. The chapter treats it as a form of self-protection: a painful fact may threaten self-image, so a more comfortable explanation is adopted. A person may genuinely experience the explanation as convincing, even when others see that it does not fit the evidence. Rationalization can prevent learning because the actual cause of a mistake, harmful decision, or broken commitment is never addressed. A critical review should compare the explanation with contemporaneous evidence, consider alternative motives, and distinguish a plausible reason from a reason that merely feels reassuring.
Study instruction:
- "Take an explanation for a disappointing outcome and test whether it is supported by records, independent observations, and alternative causes."
Key point:
- Rationalization can protect self-image while obscuring the actual cause of behaviour.
- Accurate review requires evidence that could challenge a comforting explanation.
23. Q: What is cognitive dissonance?
A: Show answer
Cognitive dissonance is the psychological discomfort associated with recognising inconsistency among beliefs, attitudes, and actions. The chapter describes how a person who sees themselves as honest may feel tension after acting dishonestly. Instead of changing the behaviour or accepting an uncomfortable conclusion about the self, the person may reinterpret another aspect of the situation—for example, revising their judgment of how enjoyable an otherwise tedious task was. Dissonance does not guarantee that people will change their beliefs in a rational direction. It can motivate learning, but it can also motivate self-justification. The key analytical question is which element is being reconsidered and whether the revision is supported by evidence.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a situation where your actions conflicted with your stated values and list the different ways you could resolve the inconsistency."
Key point:
- Dissonance is discomfort arising from perceived inconsistency among cognitions and behaviour.
- Reducing discomfort is not the same as correcting the underlying inconsistency.
24. Q: What does the Festinger and Carlsmith forced-compliance experiment illustrate about dissonance?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes participants who performed tedious tasks and were then asked to tell another participant that the experiment was enjoyable. Participants received different amounts of payment for making the claim. The group paid a small amount later rated the task more favourably than the group paid a larger amount. The interpretation presented is that a small external justification was insufficient to explain the inconsistency between the participants' conduct and self-image, so they adjusted their attitude toward the task. The example illustrates one possible route by which people reduce dissonance: changing an evaluation rather than acknowledging that they acted inconsistently. It should be treated as a classic experimental illustration, not a rule that all attitude change follows the same mechanism.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why the smaller payment created a greater need for internal justification in the interpretation of the experiment."
Key point:
- The experiment illustrates how people may change attitudes to reduce inconsistency after behaviour.
- The result is an explanatory example, not proof that all dissonance produces the same response.
25. Q: How does the desire for consistency influence commitments and decisions?
A: Show answer
People generally want their beliefs, promises, and actions to appear consistent over time. The chapter explains that commitment creates both internal pressure—because consistency supports a coherent self-image—and interpersonal pressure because others expect people to keep their word. Public declarations and pledges can therefore strengthen follow-through. The same tendency can become a trap when a person continues defending a weak position or escalating a poor decision simply because they have already invested effort, money, or reputation in it. A rational approach distinguishes a commitment worth honouring from a conclusion that should change because new evidence has emerged.
Study instruction:
- "Review one ongoing commitment and decide whether continued effort serves its original purpose or merely protects consistency with a past choice."
Key point:
- Commitments can support follow-through but can also make people defend mistaken positions.
- Consistency should not override relevant new evidence or changed circumstances.
26. Q: What is the foot-in-the-door technique, and why can a small request lead to a larger commitment?
A: Show answer
The foot-in-the-door technique begins with a small request that a person is likely to accept and then follows with a larger request. The chapter explains the process through the desire for consistency: agreeing to the first request can make the later request seem like a natural continuation of the person's earlier willingness. The technique can be used in sales, fundraising, and other influence settings. A small initial agreement does not logically oblige someone to accept a different or more demanding request. To avoid being carried by momentum, the person should assess the later request on its own merits, including its cost, purpose, evidence, and fit with current priorities.
Study instruction:
- "Identify an initial low-cost agreement and a subsequent larger request; evaluate the second request independently of the first."
Key point:
- A small initial commitment can create psychological pressure to act consistently.
- Each later request should be assessed on its own substance and consequences.
Source links:

- Burger: The Foot-in-the-Door Compliance Procedure
27. Q: What is the norm of reciprocity, and how can it shape judgment?
A: Show answer
Reciprocity is the social expectation that a benefit, favour, or concession should be returned in some way. The chapter describes it as a pervasive social norm: people often feel an obligation to express gratitude or give something back after receiving a benefit. Persuaders may exploit this expectation through unsolicited gifts, favours, or concessions that create a sense of obligation. Reciprocity can support cooperation and mutual assistance, but the perceived debt may be disproportionate to the benefit received or unrelated to the quality of the offer being made. Critical evaluation separates appreciation for a gift from the independent decision about whether a subsequent request is reasonable.
Study instruction:
- "When offered a gift or favour before a request, record the actual value of the benefit and assess the request separately."
Key point:
- Reciprocity supports social cooperation but can also be used to create undue pressure.
- A favour does not remove the need to assess a later request on its merits.
28. Q: What is the door-in-the-face technique, and how does it differ from foot-in-the-door?
A: Show answer
Door-in-the-face begins with a large request that is expected to be refused and then presents a smaller target request. The second request may seem more reasonable by comparison and can be interpreted as a concession by the requester, encouraging the recipient to reciprocate. Foot-in-the-door works in the opposite sequence: a small initial request is accepted before a larger request is made. Both techniques use sequential requests, but they rely on different psychological pressures. Neither technique establishes that the final request is objectively fair or beneficial. A recipient should compare the target request with their real needs, alternatives, and constraints rather than with the deliberately extreme first request.
Study instruction:
- "Draw the request sequence for both techniques and explain the distinct influence pressure created by each sequence."
Key point:
- Foot-in-the-door moves from a small request to a larger one; door-in-the-face moves from a large request to a smaller one.
- The final request must be evaluated independently of the sequence that made it seem attractive.
Source links:

- Burger: The Foot-in-the-Door Compliance Procedure
29. Q: What is a reciprocal concession in negotiation?
A: Show answer
A reciprocal concession occurs when one party makes a concession and the other party is then invited to respond with a concession of their own. The chapter's jury example illustrates how a proposal for another ballot can be easier to accept when the proposer also accepts a meaningful condition. In negotiations, a concession can make the other side feel that refusing to move would be unfair or uncooperative. A genuine concession may help progress, but an apparent concession can be designed to make a modest offer look unusually generous. Good negotiation practice compares the actual value of each position, makes priorities explicit, and avoids treating the mere fact of movement as proof that the deal is fair.
Study instruction:
- "For a negotiation scenario, calculate the substantive value of each concession rather than judging it by how it is presented."
Key point:
- Reciprocal concessions can help parties move from deadlock toward agreement.
- A concession's real value matters more than its contrast with an intentionally extreme opening position.
30. Q: What is the representativeness heuristic?
A: Show answer
The representativeness heuristic estimates the likelihood that a person or event belongs to a category by judging how closely it resembles a typical example of that category. It can be useful when the prototype is informative, but it can also replace probability analysis with resemblance. A person may infer that someone is a librarian because the description matches a stereotype, without checking how many librarians and salespeople exist or whether the description rules out either occupation. Representativeness therefore needs to be checked against base rates, the actual evidence, and the logical structure of the alternatives. A vivid match is not the same as a statistically sound conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "In a classification problem, identify the prototype you are using and compare it with the relevant population frequencies."
Key point:
- Similarity to a stereotype is not a substitute for probability or adequate evidence.
- Representativeness can cause both stereotyping and neglect of background rates.
31. Q: Why can stereotypes make the representativeness heuristic unreliable?
A: Show answer
A stereotype is a generalised picture of the typical features of a group. If that picture is incomplete or inaccurate, judging an individual by resemblance to it can produce a mistaken classification. The chapter notes that representativeness only has a chance of helping when the assumed prototype is itself appropriate. Stereotypes can also cause a person to overlook individual evidence, variation within a group, and alternative explanations. In professional judgment, this can affect recruitment, assessment, risk evaluation, and assumptions about competence. A fair assessment uses job-relevant or decision-relevant evidence about the individual and does not treat group resemblance as a reliable substitute.
Study instruction:
- "Take a stereotype-based inference and replace it with specific, relevant evidence that could confirm or disconfirm the classification."
Key point:
- A category prototype may be inaccurate or too crude to support a conclusion about an individual.
- Individual evidence and valid criteria should take priority over stereotypes.
32. Q: What does the Linda problem show about the conjunction fallacy?
A: Show answer
The Linda problem describes a person whose profile strongly resembles a particular social or political stereotype and asks whether she is more likely to be a bank teller or a bank teller who is also active in a specified movement. Many people prefer the more detailed description because it appears more representative of the profile. Yet a conjunction—A and B—cannot be more probable than A alone, because the conjunction requires both conditions to be true. The error occurs when narrative fit overrides the basic probability relationship. The lesson is to translate verbal alternatives into their logical structure before deciding which is more likely.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite probability options as sets or events and check whether one option contains all the conditions of another."
Key point:
- The probability of A and B cannot exceed the probability of A alone.
- A detailed, plausible story can feel more likely while being mathematically less likely.
33. Q: What is base-rate neglect?
A: Show answer
Base-rate neglect occurs when a person overlooks relevant background frequencies while judging a particular case. A description may strongly resemble a familiar type, but the relevant category may be rare compared with alternatives. The chapter's example of a shy, orderly, detail-oriented person being judged more likely to be a librarian than a salesperson illustrates how a stereotype can overshadow the fact that salespeople may be far more numerous. Base rates do not determine every individual case, but they are essential information when estimating probability. The error is to let a vivid description dominate without asking how common the competing categories are in the relevant population.
Study instruction:
- "For each probability estimate, identify the reference population and write down the prevalence or frequency of the alternatives before using case details."
Key point:
- Base rates are background frequencies that help calibrate judgments about individual cases.
- A representative description should not displace relevant population information.
34. Q: How should a person combine base rates with evidence about an individual case?
A: Show answer
A sound judgment uses both general frequencies and case-specific information. The base rate provides a starting point about how common each possibility is; the quality and diagnostic value of the individual evidence then indicate how much the starting estimate should change. A vivid description is not necessarily diagnostic if it is also common among the alternative categories. In medical, operational, or investigative decisions, it is therefore necessary to ask how often the event occurs, how reliable the observed indicator is, and whether other explanations could produce the same signal. The chapter's lesson is not to ignore specific evidence, but to avoid treating it as if background frequency did not exist.
Study instruction:
- "Build a simple two-stage estimate: start with the relevant prior frequency, then assess how strongly the observed evidence distinguishes the alternatives."
Key point:
- Base rates and case-specific evidence should be considered together.
- Evidence matters most when it genuinely distinguishes the possible explanations.
35. Q: Why can statistics mislead when they are presented without comparative context?
A: Show answer
A statistic can appear alarming or reassuring depending on the comparison point that is omitted. The chapter uses a percentage price increase to show that the same relative change can have very different practical significance when the starting amount is small or large. It also discusses claims about the number of vaccinated people in hospital: without knowing the population vaccination rate and the degree of protection, the raw count cannot establish that vaccination is ineffective. Critical interpretation asks what the denominator is, what the baseline is, which population is being compared, and whether rates or counts are being reported. Context is essential to prevent a true number from supporting a false inference.
Study instruction:
- "When reading a numerical claim, identify the numerator, denominator, baseline, time period, and relevant comparison group."
Key point:
- A statistic without its denominator or baseline can be interpreted incorrectly.
- Counts, rates, and relative changes answer different questions and should not be conflated.
36. Q: What is the availability heuristic?
A: Show answer
The availability heuristic estimates how likely or frequent an event is by how easily examples come to mind. Ease of recall can sometimes be informative because frequent events are often encountered more often. But memory accessibility is also influenced by vividness, novelty, emotional impact, media coverage, and personal experience. A dramatic event may be recalled instantly even if it is rare, while a common but less vivid event may be harder to remember. The chapter illustrates how a person's direct experience of urban life can distort an estimate of how much of a country is densely built. Availability is therefore a cue about memory, not a direct measure of objective frequency.
Study instruction:
- "For an event that feels common, compare recall-based impressions with a reliable dataset or a defined rate for the relevant population."
Key point:
- Ease of recall can be driven by vividness and exposure as well as actual frequency.
- Objective rates are needed when memory availability is a poor proxy for probability.
37. Q: How do vividness and newsworthiness distort perceived risk?
A: Show answer
Newsworthy events receive disproportionate attention because they are unusual, dramatic, visual, or emotionally compelling. Repeated exposure makes examples easier to recall, which can increase estimates of their frequency or danger even when the underlying rate has not changed. The chapter describes how accidents, tornadoes, vaccine concerns, and terrorism can attract exaggerated risk estimates when vivid stories dominate attention. A sensible risk assessment distinguishes the emotional force of an example from the statistical likelihood and severity of the event. Personal stories can reveal real harms, but they should be integrated with representative data rather than substituted for it.
Study instruction:
- "For a risk that feels unusually salient, locate the best available incidence or rate data and compare the time period and population."
Key point:
- Media salience can raise perceived likelihood without a corresponding rise in actual incidence.
- Risk assessment should combine lived examples with representative statistical evidence.
38. Q: How can availability bias affect professional and organisational decisions?
A: Show answer
Managers and professionals may overestimate risks that have recently caused a visible incident and underestimate less memorable risks that occur more frequently. A recent system outage may dominate an investment discussion even if recurring data-quality failures cause greater cumulative harm. Conversely, a rare but severe incident can be underweighted if it has not occurred in the decision-maker's experience. The chapter's framework suggests that judgment should be checked against the relevant base rate and a systematic record, not only recent memory. Incident logs, trend analysis, exposure denominators, and comparable periods can help ensure resources are allocated according to evidence rather than recency or vividness.
Study instruction:
- "Compare a recent memorable event with the full incident history and assess whether resource priorities reflect frequency, severity, and exposure."
Key point:
- Recent incidents may be more memorable than representative of the underlying risk pattern.
- Systematic records help correct memory-driven risk estimates.
39. Q: What is anchoring, and why can an initial value influence later judgments?
A: Show answer
Anchoring occurs when an initial value or piece of information influences a later estimate or judgment, even when the initial figure is arbitrary or weakly relevant. The chapter describes experiments in which different suggested starting values produced markedly different estimates of the same quantity. Anchors can influence both intuitive responses and deliberate analysis: people may treat an initial number as a hint about the correct range, then selectively generate reasons that keep later thinking near it. The effect is particularly concerning when a person has limited information, low attention, or little independent basis for an estimate. Awareness helps, but does not guarantee that the anchor can be ignored.
Study instruction:
- "Before seeing another party's proposal, make and record an independent estimate using your own evidence and assumptions."
Key point:
- An initial reference point can distort subsequent estimates even when it is arbitrary.
- Independent estimates made before exposure to an anchor can reduce its influence.
40. Q: How can anchoring affect negotiations, evaluations, and expert decisions?
A: Show answer
An opening offer can shape the range of values considered acceptable in a negotiation, even when it is deliberately extreme. Similarly, an initial grade can influence a second assessor unless the second assessment is made independently. Anchoring is not restricted to inexperienced people: experts may also be influenced when the task has uncertainty or no clear reference point. A practical control is to require assessors to document independent judgments before seeing others' decisions, use objective criteria, and explain adjustments from a baseline. In negotiation, parties should research relevant market or policy information and distinguish a plausible benchmark from a strategically chosen starting point.
Study instruction:
- "Design an evaluation process in which independent assessors make and document preliminary judgments before seeing other ratings or proposals."
Key point:
- Anchors can influence experts as well as novices, especially under uncertainty.
- Independent first estimates and documented criteria reduce dependence on prior values.
41. Q: What practical steps can reduce the influence of anchoring?
A: Show answer
No single technique reliably removes anchoring, but several steps can make it less influential. Conduct relevant research before entering a negotiation or review; record an independent estimate before seeing a proposed value; use more than one benchmark; consult people who have not been exposed to the same anchor; and allow enough time to examine assumptions. Where possible, blind a reviewer to an earlier rating or proposed outcome. The chapter warns that the effect is pervasive, so the aim should be risk reduction rather than a promise of complete immunity. A person should also check whether their reasons for accepting a value were generated independently or merely assembled to justify the initial reference point.
Study instruction:
- "Choose two independent benchmarks and explain why each is relevant before reviewing the anchor supplied by another person."
Key point:
- Preparation, independence, multiple benchmarks, and adequate deliberation can reduce anchoring.
- Anchoring is difficult to eliminate completely, so process safeguards matter.
42. Q: What is the contrast effect, and how does it differ from anchoring?
A: Show answer
The contrast effect occurs when a judgment is influenced by a nearby comparison that makes an option appear better or worse than it would when considered independently. A parking fine may seem reasonable after a larger initial fine is presented, even if the reduced amount remains substantial. Anchoring refers more specifically to the influence of an initial reference point on a later estimate; contrast concerns the way a nearby comparator changes perceived magnitude or attractiveness. The mechanisms can overlap in sequential persuasion. A good safeguard is to evaluate the actual option against objective criteria and realistic alternatives, rather than allowing an artificial comparison to define what seems normal.
Study instruction:
- "Reassess a price or proposal without the adjacent comparator and compare it with an independent market or value benchmark."
Key point:
- Contrast changes perceived value through comparison with a nearby alternative.
- An option should be assessed against an independent benchmark, not only the comparison presented.
43. Q: How can authority influence judgment as a heuristic?
A: Show answer
When time or information is limited, people may use the perceived authority of a speaker as a shortcut for judging a message. Relevant expertise can be valuable, but authority cues may be crude: status, confident delivery, institutional affiliation, or technical language can be mistaken for competence in the specific subject. The chapter describes how perceived expertise may carry greater weight when an audience is inclined toward quick processing, even when the argument itself is weak. A critical listener checks whether the person has relevant expertise, whether the evidence is sound, whether the claim lies within that expertise, and whether independent sources support the conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "For a claim from an apparent expert, verify subject-specific competence, evidence quality, conflicts of interest, and independent corroboration."
Key point:
- Expertise is relevant only when it is genuine and matched to the subject of the claim.
- A prestigious source does not make a weak argument strong.
44. Q: How do likeability and identity affect the persuasiveness of a message?
A: Show answer
People may respond more positively to a message when they like its presenter or identify with that person. The chapter notes features associated with likeability, such as perceived similarity, humour, attractiveness, or signs that the speaker likes the audience. Identity can act as a shortcut: if someone who seems similar to us supports an option, we may assume it will suit us too. These cues can influence trust and attention, but they do not establish the truth of a claim. A sound evaluation separates the relationship with the speaker from the content of the argument, while still recognising that communication style can affect whether people are willing to listen.
Study instruction:
- "Select a message from a likeable or familiar communicator and evaluate its reasoning separately from your impression of the person."
Key point:
- Likeability and shared identity can increase persuasion without strengthening evidence.
- Evaluate the message's claims independently of the social appeal of its source.
45. Q: How do authority, likeability, and identity relate to ethos, pathos, and logos?
A: Show answer
The chapter connects source-based persuasion with Aristotle's three familiar routes: ethos concerns the perceived character or credibility of the speaker, pathos concerns the emotions aroused in an audience, and logos concerns the reasoning and arguments presented. Authority, likeability, and identity are characteristics that can influence how a source is received. They may operate alongside the substance of the message rather than proving it. A persuasive speaker can use credibility and emotional connection to help an audience attend to a sound argument, but those features can also distract from weak reasoning. Critical evaluation examines all three dimensions without confusing persuasive force with logical strength.
Study instruction:
- "For a persuasive speech, identify its ethos, pathos, and logos features and assess which ones support the case and which merely attract agreement."
Key point:
- Ethos, pathos, and logos describe different routes through which communication can persuade.
- Persuasive impact and evidential strength should be assessed separately.
46. Q: What is social proof, and when can it be a useful shortcut?
A: Show answer
Social proof occurs when people use the behaviour or choices of others as a guide, particularly when they are uncertain about what is appropriate or safe. Examples include checking reviews for an unfamiliar product or observing how people behave in a new environment. Other people's actions may contain useful information when they have relevant experience and their judgments are reasonably independent. Social proof becomes unreliable when the crowd is misinformed, following a manipulated signal, copying others, or responding to the same incomplete information. Popularity can indicate visibility or conformity rather than quality. A critical consumer asks who is represented, how the opinions were formed, and whether the evidence is independent.
Study instruction:
- "Inspect a set of ratings or public opinions and determine whether they are independent, representative, recent, and relevant to the decision."
Key point:
- Observed group behaviour can inform judgment under uncertainty.
- Popularity alone does not establish accuracy, quality, or safety.
47. Q: What is groupthink, and how can social proof contribute to poor group decisions?
A: Show answer
Groupthink is a proposed pattern of group decision-making in which pressures toward agreement and cohesion can weaken critical evaluation of alternatives. Social proof may contribute when members treat apparent majority agreement as evidence that a proposal is correct. The chapter describes how a dominant leader, strong group similarity, crisis conditions, high emotions, and reluctance to dissent can narrow discussion. Information held by a minority may then be overlooked even when it would materially change the decision. The practical concern is not that groups are always irrational, but that a group can fail to surface uncertainty, test assumptions, or consider competing options when agreement becomes more important than analysis.
Study instruction:
- "Review a committee decision and identify whether alternatives, dissenting evidence, and uncertainties were genuinely examined before agreement."
Key point:
- Pressure for consensus can suppress information that would improve a group decision.
- Structured dissent and independent evidence checks help prevent premature agreement.
48. Q: What is pluralistic ignorance, and how can silence be mistaken for agreement?
A: Show answer
Pluralistic ignorance occurs when individuals privately feel uncertain or hold reservations but misread the calm or silent behaviour of others as evidence that everyone else agrees. The chapter imagines committee members who are each hesitant to question an argument but look to colleagues for signs of dissent. Because everyone is doing the same thing, the absence of visible disagreement is interpreted as support. A decision may then proceed even though uncertainty is widespread. The safeguard is to create explicit opportunities for members to state concerns independently, ask what evidence is missing, and record unresolved questions before interpreting silence as consent.
Study instruction:
- "In a group meeting, use an individual written poll or round-robin check before discussion to surface private concerns and uncertainty."
Key point:
- Silence can reflect shared uncertainty rather than genuine agreement.
- A group should solicit independent views instead of inferring consensus from outward behaviour.
49. Q: What practical controls can improve decision quality in committees and teams?
A: Show answer
A team can reduce conformity pressure by requiring independent preliminary judgments, asking a designated reviewer to challenge assumptions, inviting minority views, separating idea generation from evaluation, and recording reasons for rejecting alternatives. Leaders should make it safe to raise uncertainty and should avoid signalling a preferred answer before members have assessed the evidence. The group should identify what evidence would change its recommendation, distinguish agreement from confidence, and document unresolved risks. These controls are consistent with the chapter's warnings about social proof and pluralistic ignorance. They are process safeguards, not guarantees: a group must still examine the quality of its data, reasoning, expertise, and decision criteria.
Study instruction:
- "Draft a meeting protocol requiring independent views, a documented alternative, and explicit treatment of unresolved uncertainty."
Key point:
- Independent judgments and structured dissent help prevent conformity from replacing evaluation.
- Good process supports but cannot substitute for sound evidence and analysis.
50. Q: What are the limitations of the classic groupthink model?
A: Show answer
The chapter introduces groupthink as a useful way to think about conformity and failures to evaluate alternatives, but the concept should not be treated as an uncontested scientific law. Research reviews have questioned whether the classical model's proposed conditions reliably produce the predicted outcomes and have argued for broader models of group problem-solving. Aldag and Fuller, for example, concluded that available research did not convincingly establish the validity of the phenomenon or the claim that its characteristics necessarily lead to negative outcomes. This does not mean that conformity, silence, or poor deliberation never occur. It means that analysts should describe observable group processes and evidence rather than label every poor decision 'groupthink' without diagnosis.
Study instruction:
- "When diagnosing a poor team decision, describe the observable process and evidence before applying the groupthink label."
Key point:
- Groupthink is a useful concept but its classic causal claims have been contested.
- Precise analysis should distinguish observed conformity processes from an overbroad diagnostic label.
51. Q: What is framing in communication?
A: Show answer
Framing is a way of presenting or interpreting an issue that emphasises some aspects while giving less attention to others. The selected angle can influence what an audience notices, which comparisons seem relevant, and how an issue is understood. The chapter illustrates how the same behaviour may be described with different labels, and how political language can characterise an action in a way that supports a particular interpretation. Frames are not automatically deceptive: they can help an audience understand unfamiliar material by connecting it to known concepts. They become problematic when important facts or perspectives are deliberately obscured so that the audience accepts a distorted picture.
Study instruction:
- "Take a disputed issue and write two accurate but different frames; note what each emphasises and what each leaves out."
Key point:
- A frame selects and emphasises aspects of an issue.
- The fairness of a frame depends partly on whether it preserves relevant context rather than concealing it.
Source links:

- Chong and Druckman: Framing Theory
52. Q: What are the legitimate educational and coping uses of framing?
A: Show answer
Framing can help an audience understand unfamiliar information by comparing it with something already familiar. It can also offer a perspective that makes a difficult situation more manageable—for example, treating an examination as a challenge rather than only as a threat. These uses are legitimate when the comparison illuminates relevant features without pretending that unlike situations are identical or hiding material risks. A good educational frame helps the audience see structure, purpose, or implications that would otherwise be difficult to grasp. A coping frame may change how a person approaches a situation without changing the underlying facts. The distinction is between reframing perspective and misrepresenting reality.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate an analogy or coping frame by stating what it clarifies, what it does not imply, and which facts must remain visible."
Key point:
- Framing can support learning and coping when it clarifies rather than distorts.
- A useful analogy should illuminate selected features without concealing important differences.
Source links:

- Chong and Druckman: Framing Theory
53. Q: How can framing be used manipulatively in propaganda or persuasion?
A: Show answer
A communicator may select an emotionally powerful image or label that highlights one side of an issue so strongly that the audience struggles to remember other relevant aspects. The chapter describes this as a way propaganda can work: a vivid frame fills the imagination and narrows what seems worth considering. Such framing may rely on omission, loaded terms, selective comparisons, or an assumed cause-and-effect story. The correct response is not to reject every persuasive message, but to restore missing context. Ask which facts have been omitted, whose perspective is absent, which alternative description is defensible, and whether the frame would still seem convincing if the same evidence were described differently.
Study instruction:
- "For a persuasive message, identify its key labels, omitted facts, implied comparison, and the strongest alternative frame."
Key point:
- Manipulative framing gains influence by making a partial picture feel complete.
- Restoring omitted evidence and alternative descriptions can expose a distorted frame.
Source links:

- Chong and Druckman: Framing Theory
54. Q: How do metaphors, analogies, and allegories function as framing devices?
A: Show answer
Metaphors, analogies, and allegories explain an issue by relating it to something an audience already recognises. This can make an unfamiliar process easier to understand and can guide attention toward selected similarities. The same device can mislead if the comparison imports features that do not apply or hides decisive differences between the two situations. The chapter notes that analogical arguments will receive further treatment elsewhere in the book, but the key point here is that figurative language shapes the frame through which an audience interprets a claim. A critical reader should ask both what the comparison reveals and what it may conceal.
Study instruction:
- "For an analogy used in a speech or report, state the relevant similarity, the important differences, and the conclusion the analogy can legitimately support."
Key point:
- Figurative language helps audiences understand by mapping a familiar frame onto a new subject.
- A persuasive analogy is limited by the relevance of its similarities and differences.
Source links:

- Chong and Druckman: Framing Theory
55. Q: How does Martin Luther King Jr.'s reframing of the word 'extremist' illustrate ethical persuasion?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes how King responded to being labelled an extremist by reconsidering the meaning of the label and asking what kind of extremism should be judged. He placed his position alongside historical and religious examples and distinguished commitment to love from commitment to hate. This reframing did not simply deny the accusation; it changed the interpretive question and invited the audience to evaluate the moral direction of the commitment. The technique is selective and persuasive, but the chapter presents it as part of respectful dialogue rather than propaganda. The example shows that framing can challenge a loaded label while still making a substantive argument that an audience can examine.
Study instruction:
- "Analyse the original label, the replacement frame, the historical analogies, and the ethical claim that the reframing asks the audience to assess."
Key point:
- Reframing can challenge an unfair label by changing the question under consideration.
- Ethical persuasion remains open to scrutiny of the evidence and values supporting the new frame.
Source links:

- Chong and Druckman: Framing Theory
56. Q: What is the difference between argument and rhetoric?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes rhetoric as the art of persuasive communication and distinguishes it from argument by their primary appeal. Arguments seek to persuade through reasons and evidence and are associated with reflective System 2 assessment. Rhetoric focuses on the effectiveness of communication and often appeals to quick judgments through emotion, framing, source credibility, identity, or vivid presentation. The categories overlap: rhetorical communication may contain arguments, and arguments are rarely free from rhetorical features. The difference is therefore not that one contains logic and the other contains none. It is that rhetorical success is often measured by whether an audience is persuaded, whereas argument quality depends on the strength of the reasons offered.
Study instruction:
- "Take a persuasive message and distinguish its inferential claims from the presentation features that make the audience receptive."
Key point:
- Arguments are assessed by the quality of reasons and evidence; rhetoric is assessed by persuasive effect.
- Real communication often combines argument with rhetorical influence.
Source links:

- Frankish: Dual-Process and Dual-System Theories of Reasoning
- Chong and Druckman: Framing Theory
57. Q: Why should critical thinkers remain alert to 'System 1 candy' in arguments?
A: Show answer
The chapter uses the idea of 'System 1 candy' for attractive features of an argument's content, source, or medium that influence the audience independently of the argument's substance. These may include confident delivery, identity cues, likeability, emotional images, framing, or a memorable story. Such features can make an audience listen and may help communicate a sound argument, but they can also distract from weak evidence or invalid inference. Critical thinkers should not assume that a polished presentation is deceptive, nor that a persuasive message is necessarily sound. They should identify which features attract attention and then separately test the actual claims, evidence, and reasoning.
Study instruction:
- "Mark each persuasive feature in a message as either evidence-bearing content or a presentation cue, then assess the reasoning independently."
Key point:
- Rhetorical features can affect reception without improving the logical strength of an argument.
- Awareness allows useful communication while protecting against irrelevant persuasion cues.
Source links:

- Frankish: Dual-Process and Dual-System Theories of Reasoning
- Chong and Druckman: Framing Theory
58. Q: Can rhetoric be used ethically without weakening critical thinking?
A: Show answer
Yes. The chapter recognises that communication rarely avoids rhetoric entirely and that a speaker may legitimately use a clear analogy, appropriate emotional context, credible sources, or an accessible style so the audience is willing to listen. Ethical use helps people engage with a sound argument; it does not replace the need for accurate claims and relevant evidence. The line is crossed when presentation is used to conceal weaknesses, manipulate an audience's emotions, or suppress alternative perspectives. A responsible communicator should make the reasoning inspectable, present material limitations, and avoid implying that a source's status or a story's vividness proves a conclusion.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a difficult explanation for a non-specialist while preserving its evidence, qualifications, and relevant counterarguments."
Key point:
- Rhetoric can support understanding when it serves rather than substitutes for sound reasoning.
- Ethical persuasion remains transparent about evidence, uncertainty, and limitations.
Source links:

- Chong and Druckman: Framing Theory
59. Q: How can a person protect themselves from multiple biases when making an important decision?
A: Show answer
An effective process begins by defining the question and the decision criteria before considering attractive options. The decision-maker should record an initial independent estimate, identify relevant base rates, seek evidence that could disconfirm the preferred explanation, and separate facts from interpretations. They should also test whether vivid examples, recent events, stereotypes, authority cues, social proof, or an initial offer are shaping judgment. For group decisions, obtain independent views before discussion and invite evidence-based dissent. Finally, document uncertainty and specify what new information would trigger revision. These safeguards do not remove bias entirely, but they turn awareness into repeatable practices.
Study instruction:
- "Apply this checklist to a real decision and document how each safeguard changed or confirmed the conclusion."
Key point:
- Debiasing works best as a structured process rather than a vague intention to be objective.
- Independent information, pre-set criteria, and explicit counterevidence improve accountability.
Source links:

- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias: A Ubiquitous Phenomenon in Many Guises
60. Q: How should a critical thinker evaluate a claim made by an apparent expert?
A: Show answer
First determine whether the speaker's expertise is relevant to the specific question; authority in one field does not establish competence in another. Next examine the evidence, methods, assumptions, and reasoning offered. Check whether the speaker distinguishes established findings from opinion, discloses material conflicts, and acknowledges uncertainty. Compare the claim with independent authoritative sources, especially where the decision is consequential. Finally, consider whether presentation, status, likeability, or shared identity is influencing trust. The chapter does not argue that expert testimony should be ignored; expertise is often important evidence. It warns against allowing a crude authority cue to replace examination of what the expert actually knows and demonstrates.
Study instruction:
- "For one public claim, verify the expert's subject-specific credentials and independently inspect the evidence supporting the stated conclusion."
Key point:
- Relevant expertise is valuable but must be matched to the subject and checked against evidence.
- Trust in a source should not replace scrutiny of the claim.
61. Q: What is the relationship between cognitive biases and fallacious arguments?
A: Show answer
A fallacious argument is weak reasoning that may appear stronger than it is. Cognitive biases help explain why people accept or generate such arguments: a conclusion may feel right because it confirms an existing belief, matches a stereotype, comes from a familiar authority, or is presented through a vivid frame. The chapter positions the psychology of judgment alongside the traditional study of argument structure because identifying a formal or informal error is not always enough to explain its persuasive force. A critical thinker should therefore test the inference itself and examine the psychological cues making it attractive. A bias does not, by itself, prove that a conclusion is false; it identifies a reason to scrutinise how the conclusion was reached.
Study instruction:
- "For an argument you find persuasive, identify its inference and any psychological cues that might be making it seem stronger than it is."
Key point:
- Biases can increase vulnerability to fallacies but do not automatically determine whether a conclusion is true.
- Critical thinking evaluates both logical structure and the influences shaping acceptance.
Source links:

- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias: A Ubiquitous Phenomenon in Many Guises
62. Q: How can an organisation use the chapter's concepts to improve risk and performance decisions?
A: Show answer
An organisation can apply the chapter by designing decision processes that make evidence and challenge routine. Risk reviews should compare current incidents with base rates and exposure, not rely only on recent memorable events. Project assessments should use independent estimates before a proposed schedule or budget becomes an anchor. Performance reviews should require evidence from multiple sources to reduce self-serving explanations and halo effects from authority or likeability. Committees should seek independent views and record dissent rather than treating silence as agreement. Leaders should also frame concerns accurately and ensure that persuasive presentations do not hide uncertainty. These practices improve the information environment in which judgment occurs.
Study instruction:
- "Select one organisational decision process and identify a concrete control for confirmation bias, anchoring, base-rate neglect, and conformity."
Key point:
- Decision systems should make challenge, independence, and context part of routine governance.
- A culture of evidence-based dissent helps organisations detect assumptions and risks earlier.
Source links:

- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias: A Ubiquitous Phenomenon in Many Guises
63. Q: How should a decision-maker respond when new evidence conflicts with a previous commitment?
A: Show answer
The decision-maker should distinguish the value of keeping a promise from the separate question of whether the underlying belief or plan remains defensible. Commitments can create legitimate obligations, but they do not make an inaccurate factual assumption true. The person should identify what has changed, assess the reliability of the new evidence, consider who is affected by changing course, and explain the decision transparently. If a commitment must be revised, practical steps may include notifying stakeholders, documenting reasons, meeting any continuing obligations, and designing a responsible transition. Continuing solely to avoid admitting an earlier mistake can increase losses and compound the original error.
Study instruction:
- "Use a real or hypothetical sunk-commitment case and identify what obligations remain even if the original plan is no longer rational."
Key point:
- Consistency is valuable only when it remains compatible with evidence and legitimate obligations.
- Changing a plan in response to reliable new information can be rational and responsible.
64. Q: How can framing influence the interpretation of statistics and risk communication?
A: Show answer
A statistic never communicates itself independently of the terms, comparisons, and visual context used to present it. A communicator may emphasise relative change rather than absolute level, a dramatic individual case rather than a population rate, or benefits while leaving costs less visible. The chapter's framing discussion complements availability and base-rate neglect: a selected frame can make one aspect of the evidence memorable while obscuring the denominator or baseline needed to interpret it. A sound risk communication should state the population, time period, absolute numbers where possible, comparison group, and uncertainty. The audience should be able to reconstruct the meaning rather than rely only on the impression created by presentation.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite a statistic using absolute and relative values, an explicit denominator, a comparison period, and a clear statement of uncertainty."
Key point:
- Framing and numerical context jointly shape how risk information is understood.
- Transparent denominators and balanced comparisons help prevent misleading impressions.
Source links:

- Chong and Druckman: Framing Theory
65. Q: What should be learned from the chapter's exercises on protecting against heuristics and biases?
A: Show answer
The exercises ask learners to turn conceptual knowledge into practical safeguards. A useful response includes protections against poor group decisions, deliberate persuasion techniques, and one's own motivated reasoning. Examples include seeking disconfirming evidence, checking base rates, making independent estimates before seeing an anchor, assessing a source's actual expertise, and creating ways for dissenting views to be heard. The chapter also encourages analysis of real persuasive communication, including speeches, films, and examples selected because they are relevant to the learner's situation. The aim is applied recognition: identify the bias in context, explain how it operates, and select a proportionate correction.
Study instruction:
- "Build a personal bias-prevention checklist and test each item on one real message or decision example."
Key point:
- Bias knowledge becomes useful when translated into observable practices.
- Contextual analysis helps learners recognise how persuasion and bias operate in real decisions.
Source links:

- Nickerson: Confirmation Bias: A Ubiquitous Phenomenon in Many Guises
66. Q: What is the chapter's central lesson about becoming a more rational critical thinker?
A: Show answer
The central lesson is not that people should abandon intuition or emotion, but that they should understand the roles those processes play and recognise when they are likely to mislead. Cognitive and practical rationality require evidence-based beliefs and actions aligned with genuine goals. Heuristics make everyday judgment manageable, yet confirmation, self-serving, representativeness, availability, anchoring, and social influences can systematically distort conclusions. Framing and rhetoric affect which aspects of an issue an audience notices and how persuasive a claim feels. Better thinking therefore combines reflective analysis with habits of self-questioning, independent evidence gathering, fair representation of alternatives, and transparent communication. Critical thinking is a disciplined practice of checking both the argument and the mind receiving it.
Study instruction:
- "Summarise the links among rationality, heuristics and biases, and framing, then give one practical safeguard for each theme."
Key point:
- Rational judgment requires both sound reasoning and awareness of psychological influences.
- Reflective habits improve decision quality when they are applied to evidence, alternatives, and communication context.
Source links:

- Frankish: Dual-Process and Dual-System Theories of Reasoning
- Chong and Druckman: Framing Theory
