Learners can also investigate why some people or groups distrust expert consensus, using the psychological and social ideas introduced in this and earlier chapters. The objective is not simply to label arguments as fallacious, but to explain precisely what makes them strong, weak, legitimate, or dangerous.
Study instruction:
- "Build a small casebook of real examples and annotate each with the power type, critical questions, evidence, and conclusion-strength assessment."
Key point:
- Practice should involve real arguments and explicit evaluation, not memorising labels alone.
- The goal is informed judgement about when to rely on, question, or resist influence.

Table 7
Character, Ad Hominem, and Social Influence
1. Q: What is the central subject of Chapter 7, and how does it continue the discussion of social power in the previous chapter?
A: Show answer
Chapter 7 examines how a person's moral character, personal reputation, group membership, and social identity influence what others believe and do. It develops two main themes from the preceding discussion of authority: positive appeals to character, called ethotic arguments, and negative personal attacks, including several forms of ad hominem reasoning. It then examines referent power and ad populum arguments, which rely on the beliefs or behaviour of people with whom an audience identifies. The chapter's central concern is how to distinguish legitimate reasons for relying on people and groups from shortcuts that bypass evidence, encourage prejudice, or silence independent judgment.
Study instruction:
- Map the chapter's three broad areas—ethotic authority, ad hominem arguments, and referent power/ad populum—and identify one practical decision in which each could influence judgment.
Key point:
- Character and group identity can influence belief and action as well as formal expertise or authority.
- The task is to assess relevance, reliability, proportionality, and context rather than accept or reject personal influence automatically.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
2. Q: What is an ethotic argument?
A: Show answer
An ethotic argument uses a person's moral character as a reason to regard a claim as more or less plausible. In its positive form, the argument suggests that what a person says deserves greater consideration because the person is regarded as good, fair-minded, honest, or otherwise virtuous. In its negative form, a perceived character defect is used to reduce the plausibility of that person's claim. Character is therefore treated as evidence about the likely quality or trustworthiness of a statement. Such reasoning is not automatically sound: the relevant character trait must bear on the issue, and the conclusion must not exceed what that trait reasonably supports.
Study instruction:
- Find an example in which a speaker's reputation influences acceptance of a claim and identify the particular character trait being relied upon.
Key point:
- Ethotic reasoning connects a person's moral character with the plausibility of what that person says.
- The connection must be relevant and proportionate to the claim being assessed.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
3. Q: What is the basic structure of an ethotic argument?
A: Show answer
The structure has three parts. First, a general premise proposes that if a person has good character, that person's statement should be treated as more plausible, or that if a person has bad character, the statement should be treated as less plausible. Second, a premise attributes the relevant good or bad character to the person in question. Third, the conclusion adjusts the plausibility assigned to that person's statement. The structure identifies the reasoning being used; it does not prove that the premises are true or that the conclusion is justified. A critical thinker must still test the person's character, the relevance of the trait, and the amount of weight placed on it.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct one positive and one negative ethotic argument by writing out the general premise, the character premise, and the conclusion.
Key point:
- The argument moves from a general connection between character and credibility to a judgment about a particular speaker.
- Identifying the structure is only the beginning of evaluating its strength.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
4. Q: What is ethotic authority, and how does it differ from other forms of authority?
A: Show answer
Ethotic authority is the persuasive influence that comes from regarding someone as a good person or as an admirable moral example. It differs from expert authority, which rests on relevant knowledge or skill, and legitimate authority, which rests on a recognised role or entitlement to direct action. A person's apparent honesty, compassion, or fairness may create a favourable impression without establishing expertise in the subject under discussion. Similarly, someone may comply with a judge's lawful direction because of the judge's office rather than because of personal admiration. These forms can overlap, but they should not be treated as interchangeable when evaluating a claim.
Study instruction:
- Classify three examples of influence as primarily expert, legitimate, or ethotic authority and explain whether more than one form is operating.
Key point:
- Ethotic authority rests on perceived moral character, not simply knowledge or formal position.
- A positive moral reputation does not automatically establish subject-matter expertise.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
5. Q: Under what circumstances can a person's good character provide a reasonable reason to consider a claim more plausible?
A: Show answer
Character can provide a provisional reason for confidence when the person's demonstrated fairness, honesty, compassion, or willingness to resist pressure is relevant to the issue and little other information is available. The chapter illustrates this with a jury-room situation in which Juror 8's willingness to stand against the majority makes another juror more willing to hear him out. That personal quality does not prove the defendant's innocence; it supports giving the proposal a fair hearing. Ethotic authority is therefore most defensible as a limited heuristic in conditions of uncertainty, not as a substitute for evidence or detailed argument.
Study instruction:
- Distinguish between giving a person a fair hearing because of character and accepting that person's substantive conclusion as established.
Key point:
- Good character may justify provisional attention when evidence is limited.
- It rarely provides enough by itself to establish a complex factual conclusion.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
6. Q: What are the three critical questions for evaluating an appeal to good character?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes three questions: (1) Can the person reasonably be regarded as having good character? (2) Is that good character of the right kind to be relevant to the situation? (3) Is the weight placed on the person's character proportionate to the circumstances? The first question tests whether the positive reputation is warranted. The second guards against transferring an unrelated virtue into a different area of judgment. The third prevents a limited character-based impression from becoming an unjustified conclusion. Together, the questions help a thinker decide whether character is a useful clue, a weak consideration, or an irrelevant distraction.
Study instruction:
- Apply all three questions to a public endorsement, a workplace recommendation, or a decision to trust someone's advice.
Key point:
- Test whether the character is real, relevant, and given proportionate weight.
- Passing one question does not automatically answer the other two.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
7. Q: What should count as good moral character when evaluating an ethotic argument?
A: Show answer
Good moral character is not a single universally agreed quality. It may involve honesty, fairness, compassion, humility, courage, impartiality, or other virtues, and judgments about these qualities can be influenced by cultural and social expectations. The chapter connects relevant character traits with critical-thinking dispositions such as open-mindedness, truth-seeking, and willingness to listen. A careful evaluator should identify the actual behaviour that supports the reputation rather than relying on labels, popularity, appearance, or group stereotypes. It is also necessary to consider whether the trait has been demonstrated consistently and in circumstances relevant to the present issue.
Study instruction:
- Choose a character trait used to support someone's credibility and list the observable behaviours that would provide evidence for it.
Key point:
- Character judgments require evidence about conduct, not merely favourable labels or impressions.
- Standards of virtue can vary, so the relevant context and expectations should be made explicit.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
8. Q: Why must the character trait be relevant to the subject being discussed?
A: Show answer
A virtue in one area does not automatically establish competence or sound judgment in another. A person may be compassionate and honest but lack the technical knowledge needed to advise on engineering, medicine, or public finance. A judge's impartiality may be relevant to a legal decision, while that same person's sporting preferences would not establish expertise in athletics. Relevance protects against treating a favourable general impression as evidence for every quality or claim. The evaluator should ask what the issue requires and whether the specific trait provides a rational connection to the conclusion being considered.
Study instruction:
- For a person admired for one quality, identify a separate claim they make and test whether the admired quality has any genuine bearing on it.
Key point:
- A character trait supports a claim only when it is relevant to the issue.
- Admiration in one domain should not be transferred automatically to unrelated domains.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
9. Q: What does proportionality mean when evaluating ethotic authority?
A: Show answer
Proportionality means that the amount of confidence or influence assigned to a person's character should match the strength and relevance of the available evidence. A modest indication of fairness may justify listening carefully, but it does not justify accepting an unsupported claim without further examination. In high-stakes decisions, a character-based impression should normally carry less weight than directly relevant evidence, expertise, and verification. Where information is scarce, character may help guide an initial judgment, but the conclusion should remain provisional. The evaluator should avoid turning a limited positive impression into certainty or an outright rejection of alternatives.
Study instruction:
- Review a decision influenced by reputation and state what conclusion the character evidence can reasonably support—and what it cannot establish.
Key point:
- The strength of the conclusion should not exceed the strength of the character evidence.
- Provisional trust is different from unquestioning acceptance.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
10. Q: How does a role model illustrate ethotic power, and how is this different from following a rule?
A: Show answer
A role model influences behaviour because people admire the individual and imagine how that person would respond to a situation. Ethotic power focuses on the example of a particular person and the virtues attributed to that person. Rule-based legitimate authority, by contrast, directs behaviour through an accepted rule, office, or formal obligation. Asking what a respected role model would do is therefore different from asking what a policy requires. A role model can inspire reflection, but the example still needs to be interpreted in context; imitation should not override evidence, ethical duties, or appropriate procedures.
Study instruction:
- Compare a decision made by following a formal rule with one guided by a personal role model, and identify the different reasons for each choice.
Key point:
- Ethotic power works through admiration and personal example.
- Legitimate authority works through recognised rules or roles, even when no personal admiration exists.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
11. Q: Why can role models be persuasive when abstract moral principles are difficult to apply?
A: Show answer
A role model provides a vivid, memorable example that helps people imagine how a virtue might be expressed in a particular situation. Abstract descriptions of courage, fairness, or compassion can be difficult to translate into action, whereas a familiar person or story gives those qualities a concrete form. Asking what a respected person might do can therefore provide a starting point for deliberation. The limitation is that a role model's example is not a complete decision rule. The circumstances may differ, the model may be imperfect, and the learner must still evaluate whether the imagined response is appropriate.
Study instruction:
- Select a role model and translate one admired quality into a specific action that would be appropriate in a different real-life situation.
Key point:
- Personal examples can make abstract virtues easier to understand and apply.
- A role model should prompt reflection, not replace independent judgment.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
12. Q: How do celebrity endorsements use ethotic authority in marketing?
A: Show answer
A celebrity endorsement can transfer an audience's positive impression of a person to a product or brand. If consumers regard a celebrity as intelligent, sincere, tasteful, successful, or admirable, the association may make the product seem more attractive even when those qualities do not establish anything about the product's performance. The persuasion works through an implicit connection between the celebrity's perceived character and the brand. A critical consumer should separate the celebrity's reputation from evidence about product quality, safety, value, and suitability. Endorsement is a persuasive signal, not independent proof that the product is good.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a celebrity advertisement by listing the qualities attributed to the celebrity and the separate evidence available about the product.
Key point:
- Marketing can transfer admiration for a person to a product through association.
- Product claims still require product-specific evidence and verification.

- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
13. Q: What is the halo effect, and why does it create a risk when judging character?
A: Show answer
The halo effect is the tendency to generalise from one known positive quality to other qualities for which there is little or no evidence. Someone who is intelligent may be assumed to be morally good; an attractive person may be assumed to be more capable; or a successful public figure may be treated as trustworthy in unrelated matters. The initial favourable impression colours the interpretation of later information. This can make ethotic arguments persuasive even when the admired characteristic has no relevant connection to the claim. A critical thinker should evaluate each important quality separately rather than allow one favourable trait to determine the whole assessment.
Study instruction:
- Identify an example of a person being treated as competent in an unrelated field because of a visible success or admired characteristic.
Key point:
- The halo effect transfers a positive impression beyond the evidence that supports it.
- Assess different qualities independently and check their relevance to the decision.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
14. Q: What does the chapter's discussion of first impressions and the Asch trait-order example illustrate?
A: Show answer
The example presents two descriptions containing the same personality traits in different orders. People tend to evaluate the person described as intelligent and industrious first more favourably than the person whose description begins with negative-sounding traits, even though the lists contain the same items. The order of information changes the interpretation of ambiguous qualities that follow. This illustrates how an initial impression can frame later information and how a seemingly minor presentation choice can bias judgment. Evaluators should therefore look beyond the first description they receive and consider whether the same evidence would be interpreted differently if presented in another order.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite a person's description with the same facts in a different order and examine whether your overall impression changes.
Key point:
- The order in which information is presented can influence how later traits are interpreted.
- A first impression is not a substitute for a balanced review of the evidence.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
15. Q: What is the horn effect, and how does it relate to the halo effect?
A: Show answer
The horn effect is the negative counterpart of the halo effect. Instead of allowing one favourable characteristic to create a generally positive impression, the evaluator allows one unfavourable characteristic to colour judgments about other qualities. A person who makes one mistake may be treated as generally incompetent; someone who is unpopular may have otherwise sound contributions dismissed. Both effects encourage broad conclusions from limited information. In evaluating arguments, the horn effect can make personal attacks seem more persuasive than their relevance warrants. The evaluator should distinguish the specific weakness from the person's other qualities and assess the actual claim on its merits.
Study instruction:
- Identify a situation in which one negative event caused a broader judgment about someone's competence or credibility, then reassess the evidence separately.
Key point:
- The horn effect generalises an unfavourable impression beyond the evidence.
- Halo and horn effects both distort balanced evaluation of a person and their claims.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
16. Q: What is a direct ad hominem argument?
A: Show answer
A direct ad hominem argument attacks a person's general character in order to cast doubt on that person's view. The attack may focus on alleged dishonesty, arrogance, dogmatism, prejudice, or another perceived character defect. The reasoning is that the trait undermines the person's knowledge, judgment, willingness to tell the truth, or ability to communicate reliably. Such an argument can sometimes be relevant, but it is often used as a reactive personal attack when the speaker is under pressure. The evaluator must determine whether the alleged trait genuinely affects the claim rather than treating an insult or negative label as a refutation.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct a direct ad hominem by identifying the character accusation and the specific claim it is supposed to weaken.
Key point:
- Direct ad hominem reasoning targets general character rather than directly examining the claim's evidence.
- A personal criticism is not automatically a sound reason to reject an argument.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
17. Q: When can a person's character legitimately affect how their argument is assessed?
A: Show answer
A character trait may be relevant when it has a credible connection to the person's ability or willingness to provide reliable information. Evidence of deliberate dishonesty may matter when assessing a witness's account; a demonstrated pattern of self-serving distortion may matter when evaluating a claim about that person's own conduct. The relevant trait must be connected to the particular subject and supported by evidence. Even then, it normally changes the degree of confidence rather than proving the claim false. A critical thinker should continue to examine the evidence, corroboration, reasoning, and alternative explanations rather than rely exclusively on a character judgment.
Study instruction:
- For a disputed statement, identify what aspect of the speaker's conduct could affect reliability and what independent evidence is still needed.
Key point:
- Character can be relevant when it affects credibility or judgment in the specific context.
- A credibility concern should adjust scrutiny, not automatically decide the issue.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
18. Q: How can a direct ad hominem argument be distinguished from an abusive personal attack?
A: Show answer
A relevant direct ad hominem argument identifies a character feature that has a defensible connection to the person's reliability or judgment in the matter at hand. An abusive attack relies on insults, stereotypes, humiliation, or broad negative labels without showing that connection. For example, evidence that a witness knowingly fabricated relevant records may affect the weight given to that witness's account; calling the witness foolish or unlikeable does not establish that the account is false. The evaluator should ask what the alleged trait actually shows, how it relates to the claim, and whether the conclusion is proportionate.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite an abusive personal criticism as a precise credibility question, or explain why no relevant connection can be established.
Key point:
- Relevance and evidence distinguish a potentially useful credibility challenge from abuse.
- Insults do not substitute for an examination of the claim or its support.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
19. Q: Why might someone appeal to authority instead of taking responsibility for their own belief or action?
A: Show answer
An appeal to authority can reflect reasonable recognition that no individual can personally verify every fact or master every field. It can also be used to avoid the effort or discomfort of independent judgment, or to shield the person from responsibility for a consequential choice. Someone may say that a leader, institution, expert, or sacred authority made the decision, as though the reference removes the need to explain or defend the action. The chapter therefore distinguishes appropriate reliance on knowledge from an attempt to transfer accountability. The context determines whether referring to authority is sensible or evasive.
Study instruction:
- Examine a decision justified by saying that an authority instructed it and identify what responsibility still belongs to the decision-maker.
Key point:
- Reliance on expertise can be reasonable, but it does not automatically remove personal responsibility.
- An authority reference may conceal avoidance of independent judgment or accountability.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
20. Q: What does it mean to abrogate or shift responsibility in an appeal to authority?
A: Show answer
Shifting responsibility occurs when a person treats an authority as the decision-maker in order to avoid owning the belief or action themselves. The individual may present the authority's instruction as a complete justification, even where the individual had a duty to assess the consequences or refuse an improper direction. The chapter's examples include appeals to divine instruction and the defence of merely following orders. Such explanations can create psychological relief because someone else appears to carry the burden. A critical evaluation asks whether the situation reasonably permits reliance on the authority or requires the person to exercise independent judgment and accept responsibility.
Study instruction:
- For an instruction with significant consequences, identify who issued it, who carried it out, what checks were possible, and who remains accountable.
Key point:
- Naming an authority does not necessarily discharge the actor's own responsibility.
- Important actions require scrutiny of both the instruction and the decision to follow it.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
21. Q: What is charismatic leadership, and why can it create a risk of misplaced responsibility?
A: Show answer
Charismatic leadership involves a person being perceived as possessing exceptional or almost magical personal qualities that inspire admiration, loyalty, and a desire to follow. The leader's influence can become so strong that followers experience the leader's judgment as more certain or important than their own. This may provide reassurance and a sense of belonging, but it can also encourage followers to surrender independent judgment and allow the leader to assume excessive control. When the leader is willing to accept responsibility on behalf of followers, both sides may reinforce the arrangement. The danger is greatest when loyalty displaces scrutiny of actions, evidence, and ethical consequences.
Study instruction:
- Identify safeguards that allow an organisation to benefit from strong leadership without suspending independent review, challenge, or accountability.
Key point:
- Charisma can make a leader unusually persuasive and difficult to question.
- Loyalty should not remove the need for independent judgment and accountability.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
22. Q: What does the chapter's discussion of Milgram's obedience experiments contribute to the analysis of responsibility?
A: Show answer
The chapter notes that participants in Milgram's obedience experiments experienced reduced strain when the experimenter acknowledged that the experimenter would take responsibility for the consequences of the procedure. This illustrates how transferring responsibility to an authority can make obedience psychologically easier. It does not mean that every act of obedience is wrong or that one experiment explains all forms of compliance. The point is that a person may feel less personally accountable when a recognised authority assumes ownership of the decision. Critical evaluation should therefore consider both the authority's power and the individual's opportunity and obligation to exercise judgment.
Study instruction:
- Explain how a transfer of responsibility can change a person's willingness to comply, and identify where accountability should remain.
Key point:
- Responsibility-shifting can reduce the psychological strain associated with obedience.
- Understanding authority pressure does not by itself excuse harmful or improper conduct.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
23. Q: Which questions help evaluate whether an appeal to authority is motivated by an improper desire to avoid responsibility?
A: Show answer
The chapter suggests first asking whether the person should take direct responsibility for the belief or action, or whether referring to a particular authority is reasonable in that context. If personal responsibility should be taken, the next question is what the person stands to gain by shifting responsibility to the authority. Possible gains include avoiding blame, escaping a difficult explanation, obtaining reassurance, or protecting a position of power. These questions do not prove a motive by themselves; they identify a hypothesis that must be assessed against the circumstances and evidence. The analysis should distinguish legitimate delegation from evasive accountability-shifting.
Study instruction:
- Apply the two questions to a real or hypothetical decision and identify evidence that would support or weaken the proposed motive.
Key point:
- Ask whether personal responsibility is required and what is gained by transferring it.
- A motive-based criticism requires evidence and contextual judgment, not speculation alone.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
24. Q: Why can direct ad hominem arguments reinforce prejudice and stereotypes?
A: Show answer
Because direct ad hominem arguments judge the person rather than the content of a claim, they can draw on assumptions about race, gender, social background, religion, occupation, or other group identities. A stereotype may be used to imply that a person is dishonest, incompetent, irrational, or unworthy of attention without examining their actual conduct or evidence. Such attacks are fallacious when the group-based assumption has no legitimate connection to the person's reliability in the matter. They can also discourage people from contributing and distort whose testimony is taken seriously. Critical evaluation requires checking whether the attack is evidence-based or motivated by prejudice.
Study instruction:
- Review a credibility judgment for hidden group assumptions and identify what individual evidence would be needed instead.
Key point:
- Stereotypes can disguise prejudice as an assessment of character.
- Evaluate the individual's relevant conduct and evidence rather than group-based assumptions.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
25. Q: What should a critical thinker ask when someone launches a personal attack?
A: Show answer
The chapter highlights the question, "What is motivating this attack?" The question encourages the evaluator to examine whether the criticism responds to a genuine credibility issue or is being used to distract, intimidate, retaliate, or silence another person. It also invites self-examination: the person making the attack should consider whether their own assumptions or interests are influencing the judgment. Metacognition is important because some biases operate without conscious awareness. Listening, respect, and awareness of social-cognitive bias help prevent a personal disagreement from becoming a vehicle for unfair exclusion or prejudice.
Study instruction:
- Before accepting or repeating a personal criticism, identify the evidence, the relevance to the issue, and the possible motives or biases behind it.
Key point:
- Examine the motive and relevance of a personal attack before accepting its conclusion.
- Metacognition helps reveal biases that may otherwise remain outside conscious awareness.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
26. Q: What is a tu quoque argument, and why is it often called "whataboutery"?
A: Show answer
Tu quoque means "you too." It occurs when someone responds to a criticism by accusing the critic of the same or a similar failing instead of addressing the original point. For example, a person asked to be friendlier toward a partner's family may reply by accusing the partner of being unfriendly toward their own family. The response shifts attention from the behaviour under discussion to the critic's alleged inconsistency. It is often called whataboutery because the speaker effectively asks, "What about your own behaviour?" The move may identify a genuine double standard, but it can also be a defensive distraction.
Study instruction:
- Identify the original criticism and the counteraccusation in a tu quoque exchange, then state whether the original issue has been answered.
Key point:
- Tu quoque responds to criticism by pointing to the critic's alleged inconsistency.
- The counteraccusation does not automatically resolve the original concern.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
27. Q: Why can a tu quoque response damage the progress of a dialogue even when it contains some truth?
A: Show answer
Tu quoque is often reactive and defensive. Instead of examining the behaviour raised, the participants exchange counteraccusations and compete over who is more hypocritical. The original concern may remain unresolved, while resentment and distrust increase. Nevertheless, the response may contain relevant information if the critic is applying a standard they do not follow themselves. The evaluator must therefore avoid two extremes: treating every tu quoque as a complete refutation, or dismissing every inconsistency as irrelevant. The key is to determine whether the inconsistency bears on the claim or on the fairness of the standard being demanded.
Study instruction:
- In a disagreement, separate the original behavioural issue from any legitimate concern about inconsistent standards.
Key point:
- A defensive counterattack can derail discussion even when it identifies a real inconsistency.
- The relevance of the inconsistency determines its argumentative value.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
28. Q: When can an accusation of hypocrisy legitimately weaken a person's position?
A: Show answer
Hypocrisy can matter when the inconsistency is relevant to the claim, the standard being promoted, or the person's credibility in the particular discussion. A person who publicly condemns a practice while privately engaging in the same practice may provide a relevant basis for questioning the sincerity or consistency of their position. However, inconsistency does not necessarily make the underlying proposition false. A person can fail to follow sound advice while the advice remains correct. The evaluator should distinguish criticism of the speaker's conduct from evaluation of the claim itself and should not use hypocrisy as an automatic reason to reject a proposition.
Study instruction:
- For a hypocrisy allegation, state separately what it shows about the speaker and what it does—or does not—show about the truth of the claim.
Key point:
- Hypocrisy may affect credibility or the fairness of a demand without disproving the underlying proposition.
- The relevance of the inconsistency must be demonstrated.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
29. Q: Why does the chapter's example of a parent who smokes but warns a teenager against smoking show that tu quoque is context-dependent?
A: Show answer
The teenager's response that the parent is not entitled to warn against smoking may overlook the parent's experience and regret. The parent may be warning the teenager precisely because they understand the harm and difficulty of stopping. In that situation, the parent's behaviour does not necessarily undermine the warning; it may explain why the warning is important. The example shows that inconsistency is not always a reason to dismiss advice. The evaluator must examine the person's reasons, the relevance of their experience, and whether the advice is supported independently of the person's conduct.
Study instruction:
- Compare a hypocritical warning with a warning based on regret or experience and identify what evidence distinguishes the two.
Key point:
- A person's failure to follow advice does not automatically make the advice unsound.
- Context can explain why personal experience strengthens rather than weakens a warning.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
30. Q: What is the central critical question for assessing a tu quoque attack?
A: Show answer
The key question highlighted in the chapter is whether the attack is relevant to the claim being made. If the critic's inconsistency has no meaningful bearing on the issue, the counteraccusation is likely to distract from the original argument. If the inconsistency directly concerns the standard being imposed, the person's credibility, or the conduct they are advocating, it may be relevant. The evaluator should specify exactly what the inconsistency establishes and avoid extending it beyond that point. This prevents a legitimate concern about hypocrisy from becoming a general-purpose excuse for ignoring criticism.
Study instruction:
- Write one sentence explaining the connection between the alleged inconsistency and the original claim; if no connection can be shown, treat the tu quoque as a distraction.
Key point:
- Relevance, not the mere existence of inconsistency, determines the force of a tu quoque argument.
- A relevant criticism should be kept within the limits of what it actually establishes.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
31. Q: How does reciprocity influence the use and evaluation of tu quoque arguments?
A: Show answer
People commonly expect others to apply standards to themselves as well as to those they criticise. This expectation of reciprocity can make hypocrisy especially damaging in relationships and groups. If one person demands patience, fairness, or openness while refusing to practise those qualities, the other person may reasonably question the fairness of the demand. At the same time, reciprocal expectations can be used defensively to avoid valid feedback. A sound response recognises legitimate concerns about double standards while preserving the obligation to examine the original behaviour and make appropriate improvements.
Study instruction:
- Identify a standard expected of others and assess whether you apply the same standard consistently to your own conduct.
Key point:
- Reciprocity supports fair and consistent expectations.
- It should not become an excuse to avoid valid criticism or corrective action.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
32. Q: How can repeated tu quoque responses create a conspiracy of silence or strengthen groupthink?
A: Show answer
If people expect that raising a concern will result in a counterattack about their own imperfections, they may stop offering constructive criticism. Individuals may remain silent to avoid conflict, embarrassment, or having attention redirected toward their own behaviour. As fewer people challenge weak reasoning, the group can develop an illusion of unanimity and become less willing to examine alternatives. This can reinforce groupthink: poor arguments remain unchallenged because the social cost of speaking up is perceived to be too high. A healthier group separates the validity of feedback from the personal perfection of the person giving it and examines each concern on its merits.
Study instruction:
- Review a group decision and identify whether criticism was answered with evidence or deflected through attacks on the critic.
Key point:
- Punishing the messenger discourages future feedback and weakens group deliberation.
- Groups need ways to examine criticism without demanding that critics be flawless.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
33. Q: What is a guilt-by-association argument?
A: Show answer
Guilt by association occurs when negative qualities of a person or organisation connected with a speaker are transferred to that speaker in order to discredit the speaker's position. The association may be professional, political, social, institutional, or personal. The argument assumes that because the speaker is linked to a disreputable person or group, the speaker's claim should also be treated as disreputable. The reasoning is weak when the connection is incidental or has no bearing on the claim. It may have force when the association reveals a relevant conflict of interest, shared commitment, or involvement that genuinely affects credibility.
Study instruction:
- Identify the association being used in an argument and state what specific feature of the association is supposed to affect credibility.
Key point:
- Guilt by association transfers a negative judgment from a connected person or group to the speaker.
- The connection must have a relevant bearing on the claim to support the criticism.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
34. Q: When can an association with a person or organisation legitimately affect the assessment of a claim?
A: Show answer
An association can be relevant when it reveals something material about the speaker's interests, commitments, reliability, or role in the matter. For example, membership in an organisation that actively promotes discrimination may be relevant when evaluating a politician's position on equal treatment, depending on the nature of the membership and the claim. By contrast, merely sharing a photograph, attending the same event, or knowing a controversial person may not establish agreement or responsibility. The evaluator should establish the nature of the relationship, whether it is voluntary and current, and how it connects to the particular proposition before drawing a conclusion.
Study instruction:
- For an association-based criticism, distinguish evidence of shared commitment or involvement from mere proximity or incidental contact.
Key point:
- Association matters only when its nature and relevance are established.
- Proximity alone does not prove shared beliefs, conduct, or responsibility.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
35. Q: How can a critical thinker test a guilt-by-association argument before accepting it?
A: Show answer
The evaluator should establish whether the association is real, accurately described, and relevant to the issue. They should ask what the connection actually shows: shared membership, financial support, coordinated action, personal acquaintance, or something weaker. Next, they should consider whether the associated person's or group's conduct bears on the speaker's credibility or on the truth of the claim. Alternative explanations should be considered, including whether the association is being used simply to trigger a negative emotional reaction. The final conclusion should be limited to what the evidence supports and should not treat a vague connection as proof of guilt or falsehood.
Study instruction:
- Apply a four-step check: verify the connection, describe its nature, test its relevance, and limit the conclusion to the evidence.
Key point:
- Verify the relationship before judging it.
- A negative association is not a substitute for evaluating the claim itself.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
36. Q: What is referent power?
A: Show answer
Referent power is influence arising from the commitments, attitudes, or behaviour of people whom an individual regards as similar to themselves or identifies with as a group. It can operate through peer pressure, professional identity, community membership, or a shared social identity. A person may adopt a view or behaviour because people in a valued group appear to support it. The influence is not necessarily based on expertise or formal authority; it comes from the importance of belonging, identification, and the desire to remain accepted by the group. It can guide behaviour in uncertain situations but can also discourage independent judgment.
Study instruction:
- Identify a group whose opinions influence your decisions and explain what makes that group's approval or behaviour important to you.
Key point:
- Referent power works through identification with people regarded as "like us".
- Group influence can guide choices but may also create pressure to conform.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
37. Q: What is an ad populum argument, and what is its basic reasoning structure?
A: Show answer
An ad populum argument appeals to the fact that a belief is widely held as a reason to accept that belief as true or plausible. Its basic structure is: a large majority or everyone believes proposition X; when something is generally accepted, it is likely to be true; therefore X is true or plausible. The structure identifies popularity as evidence. Whether the inference is strong depends on the subject, the people whose beliefs are being counted, the reliability of their judgment, and the degree of certainty claimed. Popularity alone is not a universal test of truth.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct a statement beginning "everyone knows" by identifying the popularity premise, the reliability assumption, and the conclusion.
Key point:
- Ad populum reasoning uses widespread belief as support for a conclusion.
- Its strength depends on whether the relevant population is a reliable guide in that domain.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
38. Q: Why are ad populum arguments not automatically fallacious?
A: Show answer
A widely held belief can sometimes be informative, especially where people have relevant shared experience or where a community has developed practical knowledge about its own circumstances. Local knowledge, jury deliberation, employee consultation, and democratic decision-making can all give collective views a legitimate role. However, popularity may be a poor guide when the issue requires specialist knowledge, when the group is misinformed, or when social pressure suppresses disagreement. The correct conclusion may be only that a claim deserves investigation or is plausible, not that it is certainly true. The evaluator must assess the domain and the population rather than reject or accept popularity mechanically.
Study instruction:
- Compare one case where collective experience is useful with one where technical expertise or independent evidence should carry greater weight.
Key point:
- Collective belief can be informative in appropriate domains and under reliable conditions.
- Popularity is not a universal substitute for expertise or evidence.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
39. Q: What five critical questions should be asked when evaluating an ad populum argument?
A: Show answer
The chapter proposes asking: (1) Is the proposition in fact generally believed? (2) Is this a domain in which popular belief is relevant to truth? (3) Does the cited population have appropriate knowledge of the subject? (4) Is that group reliable, or are its views affected by groupthink, fear, competition, or incentives to mislead? (5) Is the conclusion suitably cautious given the evidence? These questions test the existence of consensus, its relevance, the group's competence and reliability, and the strength of the resulting conclusion. They prevent a vague appeal to "everyone" from being treated as decisive evidence.
Study instruction:
- Use all five questions to assess a public claim that relies on majority opinion or community consensus.
Key point:
- Check the existence, relevance, competence, reliability, and proper strength of the claimed consensus.
- A sound evaluation may support only a provisional or limited conclusion.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
40. Q: How should a critical thinker verify the claim that "everyone believes" something?
A: Show answer
The first task is to determine whether the asserted consensus actually exists. A confident statement that "everyone knows" may be an exaggeration designed to intimidate dissenters. The evaluator can check reliable surveys, ask a sufficiently broad and relevant group, examine representative evidence, or pause to consider whether the claim reflects only a narrow social circle. They should also distinguish a genuine majority from a highly vocal minority. Without verification, the popularity premise may be false, and the rest of the argument has no reliable foundation.
Study instruction:
- For a claim about public or group opinion, identify the population, the evidence of its views, and whether the evidence is representative.
Key point:
- A claim of consensus must be verified rather than accepted because it is confidently stated.
- A vocal group is not necessarily representative of the wider population.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
41. Q: In which areas can popular or community knowledge provide useful evidence?
A: Show answer
Collective experience can be informative about matters that members of a community have had repeated opportunities to observe. The chapter gives examples such as local knowledge about neighbourhood conditions and practical judgments about familiar services. It also discusses the role of juries, employee consultation, and democratic decision-making, where the experiences and values of participants may be relevant. Such knowledge is not infallible, but it can provide evidence that an outsider or isolated specialist might miss. The evaluator should still ask whether the group has actually encountered the issue and whether its views are open to correction and disagreement.
Study instruction:
- Identify a local or organisational decision where the experience of affected people is relevant and specify what further evidence would strengthen their input.
Key point:
- Collective experience can contribute valuable local and practical knowledge.
- Its value depends on the group's actual experience and the openness of its deliberation.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
42. Q: Why is popular belief often a weak guide to scientific or highly specialised questions?
A: Show answer
Many scientific and technical questions require methods, evidence, and expertise that are not distributed evenly across the general population. A large number of people may share a belief because of tradition, incomplete information, political identity, or repeated exposure to persuasive messages rather than because they have evaluated the evidence. Public opinion can therefore differ from expert knowledge. The chapter uses disagreement over climate change as an example of the gap that can arise. The proper response is not to assume experts are always correct, but to assess the relevant evidence and the competence and reliability of the people whose views are being cited.
Study instruction:
- Choose a scientific or technical claim and distinguish public opinion data from the relevant specialist evidence and expert assessment.
Key point:
- Popularity does not establish a scientific or technical proposition.
- Evaluate the evidence and relevant expertise rather than relying on the size of a majority alone.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
43. Q: How should the reliability of a group be assessed when its members share a belief?
A: Show answer
A group may be unreliable if its members are discouraged from dissenting, rewarded for conformity, motivated by competition, afraid of rejection, or exposed to the same misleading information. The chapter warns that social media groups and tightly aligned communities can develop dogmatic or antagonistic patterns that undermine honest communication. The evaluator should look for independent checking, willingness to acknowledge uncertainty, diversity of relevant experience, and openness to counterevidence. A large group can repeat the same error if its members depend on one another rather than independently examining the issue. Consensus is more informative when the process that produced it is trustworthy.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a group's decision process by checking how disagreement is handled, whether evidence is independently examined, and whether members can revise their views.
Key point:
- Group size does not guarantee group reliability.
- Independent scrutiny and freedom to disagree strengthen the value of collective judgment.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
44. Q: What is the difference between following a majority's belief and following a majority's decision?
A: Show answer
Following a majority's belief means treating the fact that many people think a proposition is true as a reason to believe it. Following a majority's decision means acting in accordance with a collective choice even if one does not personally agree with the underlying belief. The chapter illustrates the distinction with democratic procedures and a jury-room bargaining proposal: someone may agree to follow a decision for practical reasons without changing their own assessment of the evidence. Collective action can require coordination, while belief should remain responsive to evidence. Confusing the two can make compliance appear to be proof of conviction.
Study instruction:
- Identify a situation in which you would accept a collective decision for practical reasons while retaining a different personal view, and explain why.
Key point:
- Agreement to act with a group is not necessarily agreement with the group's belief.
- Practical coordination and truth assessment involve different questions.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
45. Q: What are social dilemmas, and why can they create tension between personal belief and action?
A: Show answer
A social dilemma arises when a person may believe that a particular action is right or beneficial but has reason to doubt that enough others will take the same action. The individual may fear being the only person who bears the cost, or may believe that isolated effort will have little effect unless others participate. The chapter uses collective environmental action as an example: an individual's reduction in emissions may seem ineffective if others do not also commit. Such situations can make coordination and shared expectations important. They also create pressure to conform or to revise beliefs in order to avoid the discomfort of acting differently from one's group.
Study instruction:
- Analyse a collective-action problem by identifying the individual incentive, the shared outcome, and the coordination needed to make action effective.
Key point:
- Social dilemmas arise when individually rational choices can undermine a collective objective.
- Coordinated action may be needed even when participants retain different personal beliefs.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
46. Q: How does social identity make referent power persuasive?
A: Show answer
Social identity consists of aspects of how people understand themselves through the groups to which they belong. These groups can provide belonging, status, shared values, and a sense of who "we" are. When a valued group appears to support a belief or behaviour, members may feel pressure to follow it because disagreement could threaten acceptance or self-esteem. The influence can be especially strong when the issue is uncertain or when the group is important to the person's identity. A critical thinker should recognise this emotional and social pressure while still assessing whether the group's position is well supported.
Study instruction:
- Identify a group identity that affects your choices and examine whether your view is based on evidence, shared values, or a desire for acceptance.
Key point:
- Group identity can make shared beliefs and behaviours unusually influential.
- Belonging should not prevent independent assessment or respectful dissent.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
47. Q: How can social media amplify ad populum pressure and create a rush to judgment?
A: Show answer
Social media can make a large or vocal reaction appear to represent a settled public consensus. Short posts, rapid sharing, emotional language, and visible approval or condemnation can reward immediate judgment while leaving little space for nuance, context, or verification. The chapter discusses a high-profile controversy in which online pressure was criticised for contributing to a rapid institutional response. The lesson is not that public criticism is always unjustified, but that online volume and intensity do not establish the accuracy or fairness of a judgment. Serious decisions require evidence, context, proportionate procedures, and an opportunity to respond.
Study instruction:
- Before sharing or endorsing an online accusation, verify the underlying facts, identify missing context, and distinguish the number of reactions from the quality of evidence.
Key point:
- A social-media storm can magnify apparent consensus and accelerate judgment.
- Online popularity should not replace careful investigation and fair process.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
48. Q: What is the difference between a descriptive norm and an injunctive norm?
A: Show answer
A descriptive norm communicates what people commonly do, while an injunctive norm communicates what behaviour is approved or disapproved. For example, telling households that their energy use is above the neighbourhood average describes what others consume. Adding a signal of approval for lower consumption or disapproval for higher consumption communicates a social judgment about the behaviour. The distinction matters because a descriptive comparison alone can influence people in unintended directions: those using less than average may increase their consumption toward the norm. Combining descriptive information with an appropriate injunctive message can reinforce the intended behaviour.
Study instruction:
- Identify the descriptive and injunctive elements in a behaviour-change message and explain what each communicates to its audience.
Key point:
- Descriptive norms show what people do; injunctive norms show what people approve or disapprove.
- The two signals can have different and sometimes opposing behavioural effects.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
49. Q: What does the energy-consumption study discussed in the chapter illustrate about social proof?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a study in San Marcos, California, in which households received information comparing their electricity consumption with the neighbourhood average. The comparison reduced consumption among households using more than average but led some lower-consuming households to increase their use. The pattern was described as a "rush to the middle": people moved toward the behaviour presented as normal. The study illustrates that a descriptive norm can influence behaviour without necessarily moving everyone toward the desired outcome. A communication strategy should therefore consider how different audiences may interpret the comparison rather than assume that a social average always produces improvement.
Study instruction:
- Explain why a message that reports an average may encourage both improvement and deterioration, depending on the recipient's starting position.
Key point:
- Social comparison can pull behaviour toward a perceived group norm.
- A descriptive average is not automatically a desirable target for every individual.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
50. Q: Why can adding an injunctive norm change the effect of a descriptive social comparison?
A: Show answer
Adding an injunctive signal communicates which side of the comparison is socially approved. In the chapter's example, households with lower-than-average consumption received a positive signal, while higher-consuming households received a negative one. This helped avoid encouraging already efficient households to increase their use simply because the average was higher. The combined message provides both information about what others do and guidance about which behaviour is valued. The effect still depends on whether the audience accepts the underlying norm and identifies with the messenger. Behavioural messages should therefore be tested for unintended effects across different groups.
Study instruction:
- Design a norm-based message that rewards the desired behaviour without implying that an undesirable group average is the target.
Key point:
- Injunctive information can clarify which behaviour is approved when a descriptive norm is ambiguous.
- Messages should be tested for unintended effects on different segments of the audience.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
51. Q: How can a social-norm message provoke resistance when it conflicts with a person's group identity?
A: Show answer
A norm message may be rejected when the audience does not identify with the group or values represented by the messenger. The chapter notes that the energy-saving approach could have the opposite effect for some customers with a non-progressive political leaning, who may react against a message perceived as expressing the values of a different social group. The point is that social influence is not uniform: people may comply with a norm that feels like "people like us" but resist one associated with an out-group. Effective communication should understand the audience's identity and avoid assuming that one persuasive frame will work equally well for everyone.
Study instruction:
- Evaluate a behaviour-change campaign from the perspective of different audience groups and identify possible identity-based resistance.
Key point:
- Norm messages can trigger reactance when the audience rejects the identity or values they appear to represent.
- Audience identification affects whether social proof encourages cooperation or defiance.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
52. Q: Why does uncertainty increase reliance on referent power and social proof?
A: Show answer
When people are unsure how to behave, they may look to others whom they regard as similar to themselves for guidance. The behaviour of peers can provide a quick benchmark when formal rules or clear evidence are absent. The chapter connects this tendency with an adaptation of Milgram's obedience experiments in which two confederates rebelled and the remaining participant's obedience rate dropped sharply. The example suggests that observing others resist an authority can make independent action easier. Social proof is therefore shaped not only by majority behaviour but also by visible dissent, group identification, and the level of uncertainty in the situation.
Study instruction:
- Identify a situation in which uncertainty makes people look to peers and consider how visible, responsible dissent could change the group's behaviour.
Key point:
- Uncertainty can increase dependence on the behaviour of similar others.
- Visible dissent can weaken conformity and make independent judgment more possible.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
53. Q: How can a person resist peer pressure without dismissing useful feedback from other people?
A: Show answer
Resistance to peer pressure requires courage and willingness to take responsibility for one's commitments, but it should not become automatic rejection of other people's views. A person may need to challenge a group when its consensus is unsupported, coercive, or unreliable. At the same time, colleagues, friends, and relatives may notice patterns of behaviour that the individual does not recognise. The chapter therefore emphasises receptiveness to feedback as well as independence. The task is to evaluate the quality, consistency, and relevance of the feedback rather than accept it solely because a group agrees or reject it merely because it is uncomfortable.
Study instruction:
- Compare feedback from several independent people with a single emotionally charged criticism and identify what evidence or pattern deserves further attention.
Key point:
- Independence requires the courage to question group pressure.
- Humility and open-mindedness require taking relevant feedback seriously.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
54. Q: Why can unanimous feedback from different people be more significant than one isolated criticism?
A: Show answer
A single criticism may reflect misunderstanding, personal conflict, or an unusual perspective. When people from different relationships and settings independently identify a similar pattern, the convergence may provide stronger reason to examine the concern. It still does not prove that every criticism is accurate, but dismissing all of it may indicate a lack of humility, courage, or open-mindedness. The chapter uses this point to show that other people may sometimes recognise aspects of our behaviour more clearly than we do. A responsible response is to ask for concrete examples, look for recurring patterns, and consider what change may be appropriate.
Study instruction:
- When receiving repeated feedback, record the specific behaviours described and look for patterns across independent sources before deciding how to respond.
Key point:
- Converging feedback can reveal patterns that an individual may not notice.
- Consider the evidence and examples rather than accepting or dismissing feedback wholesale.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
55. Q: How should a manager apply the chapter's ideas when evaluating a respected employee's recommendation?
A: Show answer
The manager should recognise that the employee's reputation may be a useful reason to listen, but should not allow admiration to replace examination of the recommendation. The manager should identify whether the employee's character is relevant, whether the person has expertise in the subject, whether any conflicts of interest exist, and whether the proposal is supported by evidence. If the employee is admired for fairness or courage, those traits may help explain why the recommendation deserves attention, but technical assumptions and likely consequences still need review. This approach preserves the value of trust while guarding against halo effects and misplaced authority.
Study instruction:
- Use the three ethotic questions and a separate evidence review when assessing a recommendation from someone with a strong personal reputation.
Key point:
- Respect and trust can justify attention, not automatic approval.
- Review character, expertise, conflicts, evidence, and consequences separately.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
56. Q: How should an organisation respond when employees use tu quoque attacks to avoid performance feedback?
A: Show answer
Managers should separate the employee's response from the original performance concern. If the employee identifies a genuine inconsistency in how standards are applied, that concern should be examined fairly. However, it should not automatically cancel the need to address the employee's own behaviour. The manager can acknowledge the alleged double standard, clarify the standard that applies to everyone, and return to the evidence about the specific performance issue. Organisations should also model reciprocal accountability by applying expectations consistently to managers and employees. This reduces the incentive to use personal counterattacks as a substitute for constructive discussion.
Study instruction:
- Prepare a feedback response that acknowledges any legitimate fairness concern while keeping the original performance issue open for resolution.
Key point:
- A valid concern about inconsistent standards should be investigated, not used to erase unrelated accountability.
- Consistent expectations and evidence-based feedback reduce defensive whataboutery.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
57. Q: How can an organisation use the chapter's analysis of referent power to improve behaviour-change communication?
A: Show answer
An organisation should identify the groups employees actually identify with, determine which behaviours are already common, and distinguish descriptive information from statements of approval. Messages should avoid presenting an undesirable average as though it were a target, because lower-performing or better-performing groups may move toward it in unintended ways. The organisation should test whether the messenger is trusted and whether the message is perceived as representing an in-group or an out-group. Feedback and monitoring should examine different audience segments, not only the average result. Norm-based communication should support clear objectives and evidence-based controls rather than rely on social pressure alone.
Study instruction:
- Design a workplace norm message and test its likely effect on employees with different starting behaviours and group identities.
Key point:
- Social norms can influence behaviour, but the effect depends on the message and the audience.
- Combine descriptive information, clear approval signals, and monitoring for unintended outcomes.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
58. Q: How can a critical thinker distinguish legitimate reliance on a respected group from conformity driven by fear of rejection?
A: Show answer
Legitimate reliance occurs when the group has relevant knowledge, a trustworthy process, and reasons that can be examined. Conformity driven by fear occurs when a person agrees or remains silent mainly to avoid embarrassment, exclusion, or conflict. The chapter notes that phrases such as "everyone knows" can imply that dissenters are ignorant or unreasonable, making it difficult to defend even a well-founded position. A critical thinker should ask whether the group is competent, whether members can disagree safely, and whether the conclusion follows from evidence. The presence of social pressure should be treated as a reason for closer scrutiny, not as proof that the majority is wrong.
Study instruction:
- Identify the evidence for a group's conclusion and separately identify any social cost attached to disagreeing with it.
Key point:
- Reliable consensus depends on knowledge and open deliberation, not merely social pressure.
- Fear of exclusion can produce outward agreement without genuine belief.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
59. Q: What integrated method can be used to evaluate arguments based on character, personal criticism, or popular opinion?
A: Show answer
First identify the kind of influence being used: positive moral character, a negative character attack, an accusation of hypocrisy, guilt by association, or appeal to group belief. Then reconstruct the actual claim and identify the evidence offered. For ethotic arguments, test whether the character is established, relevant, and proportionate. For ad hominem and tu quoque arguments, test whether the personal criticism bears on the claim and whether it is being used to avoid the issue. For ad populum arguments, verify the consensus, assess the domain and competence of the group, examine its reliability, and calibrate the conclusion. Finally, consider social pressure, identity, and the possibility of prejudice or groupthink.
Study instruction:
- Apply this sequence to a real public or workplace argument and record the type of appeal, its premises, the critical questions, and a proportionate conclusion.
Key point:
- Identify the appeal before evaluating its relevance and evidential strength.
- The conclusion should reflect the quality of the source, reasoning, context, and available evidence.
Source links:
- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
60. Q: What is the chapter's overall lesson about character, ad hominem arguments, and social influence?
A: Show answer
The chapter's central lesson is that personal character and group influence are unavoidable parts of human judgment, but they must be handled carefully. Good character can provide a provisional reason to listen, while relevant evidence about a person's conduct can sometimes affect credibility. Direct ad hominem attacks, tu quoque responses, and guilt by association become weak or harmful when they rely on prejudice, irrelevant connections, or defensive distraction. Ad populum arguments can contribute useful knowledge when the group is relevant and reliable, but can also silence dissent or amplify error. Sound judgment combines independence with humility: it resists blind conformity while remaining open to evidence and feedback from others.
Study instruction:
- Summarise the chapter's main forms of personal and group influence, then identify one safeguard against over-reliance and one safeguard against reflexive rejection for each form.
Key point:
- Evaluate character and consensus by relevance, reliability, context, and proportionality.
- Independent judgment is strongest when combined with humility, courage, and openness to well-founded feedback.

- Chapter 7: Arguments, Social Power, and Message Source (Part 2)
