Table 9
Arguments from Analogy: Structure, Evaluation, Persuasion, and Critical Practice
1. Q: What is an argument from analogy, and how does it support a conclusion?
A: Show answer
An argument from analogy supports a claim about one case by comparing it with another case that shares relevant features. It reasons that because a feature or consequence is present in the familiar or better-understood analogue case, it is likely to be present in the primary case as well. The comparison is not sufficient merely because two things look alike. The shared features must be relevant to the target conclusion, and important differences must not undermine the inference. Analogies can clarify difficult issues, but their argumentative strength depends on the quality of the comparison.
Study instruction:
- Identify the two cases, the shared features, and the conclusion in an everyday analogy.
Key point:
- An analogy transfers a claim from one case to another through relevant similarity.
- Similarity alone does not prove that the conclusion is true.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
2. Q: What is the basic logical structure of an argument from analogy?
A: Show answer
The basic structure contains two premises and a conclusion. First, primary case X is comparable to analogue case Y in relevant respects. Second, a feature Z is true of Y. The conclusion is that Z is therefore likely to be true of X. The inference is usually inductive rather than deductively conclusive: the premises may make the conclusion more plausible without guaranteeing it. A critical thinker should therefore ask what makes the cases comparable, why Z is connected to the similarities, and whether the evidence supports the degree of confidence expressed in the conclusion.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite an analogy in the form X resembles Y, Z is true of Y, therefore Z is likely to be true of X.
Key point:
- The analogue supplies the starting point for the inference.
- The conclusion is only as strong as the relevant similarities and supporting premises.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
3. Q: What additional premises make an argument from analogy more explicit?
A: Show answer
A fuller reconstruction identifies the features that the cases share and explains why those features matter. It can state that Y has features a, b, and c; X is comparable to Y with respect to those features; Z is true of Y; and Z is linked to a, b, and c. The conclusion then proposes that Z is also likely to be true of X. This reconstruction exposes gaps that a brief comparison may conceal. If the arguer cannot explain the connection between the shared features and Z, the analogy may be rhetorically attractive but evidentially weak.
Study instruction:
- Expand a short analogy into separate premises and identify the premise that connects the similarities to the target feature.
Key point:
- Explicit premises reveal the reasoning that a comparison leaves unstated.
- A relevant link between similarity and conclusion is essential.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
4. Q: What is the primary case in an argument from analogy?
A: Show answer
The primary case is the issue or entity that the conclusion is actually about. It is the case whose feature, status, treatment, or likely outcome the arguer wants the audience to accept. In an argument about whether a person should accept a demanding public role, for example, the decision under discussion is the primary case. Identifying it prevents the evaluator from being distracted by the more vivid comparison. The critical task is to determine exactly what is being claimed about the primary case and whether the comparison genuinely supports that claim.
Study instruction:
- Read an analogy and state the real-world decision or proposition that the conclusion addresses.
Key point:
- The primary case is the subject of the conclusion.
- Evaluation must return to the claim actually being made about it.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
5. Q: What is the analogue case, and why must it be identified accurately?
A: Show answer
The analogue case is the comparison used to illuminate or support the primary case. It may be a historical event, a familiar practice, an established example, or an invented scenario. Its role is to supply a feature or judgment that the arguer wants to transfer. The evaluator should identify what is actually known about the analogue rather than relying on the emotional impression created by its description. If the analogue is misunderstood, factually inaccurate, or selectively presented, the reasoning built on it may be unreliable even when the comparison sounds compelling.
Study instruction:
- In three examples, label the analogue case and verify which claims about it are factual and which are interpretations.
Key point:
- The analogue is the comparison source, not the issue being decided.
- Its facts and description must be checked independently.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
6. Q: What is the target feature in an argument from analogy?
A: Show answer
The target feature is the particular property, judgment, consequence, or relationship that the arguer wants to transfer from the analogue to the primary case. It is more precise than saying that two cases are generally similar. For example, an argument may compare two decisions because both involve accepting a public responsibility despite personal reluctance. The target feature is the alleged relevance of duty, not every characteristic of the two people or situations. Naming the target feature helps the evaluator test whether the similarities actually support the conclusion and whether the arguer has transferred more than the comparison warrants.
Study instruction:
- State the target feature in one sentence before assessing any other part of an analogy.
Key point:
- The target feature is what the analogy seeks to establish about the primary case.
- Precise identification prevents vague or excessive conclusions.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
7. Q: What are real-life or historical analogies?
A: Show answer
Real-life analogies compare a current or disputed case with historical events or other accepted truths that an audience already knows. They are commonly described as inductive analogies because evidence from an actual case is used to support a probable conclusion about another case. Their strength depends on the accuracy of the historical account, the relevance of the similarities, and the significance of the differences. A historical precedent can illuminate a present decision, but the fact that two events share some features does not mean they will have the same causes, consequences, or moral status.
Study instruction:
- Select a historical precedent used in a current debate and list both the similarities and the contextual differences.
Key point:
- Historical analogies draw on actual events or accepted facts.
- Precedent is informative only when the comparison is relevant and accurate.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
8. Q: What are hypothetical analogies, and how do they differ from historical analogies?
A: Show answer
Hypothetical analogies compare the primary case with an invented situation designed to make a particular feature easier to examine. They do not depend on the analogue having occurred in history. Their value often lies in isolating a principle, testing consistency, or helping people consider an issue from a different perspective. A hypothetical case can be vivid and educational, but its assumptions must be examined. If the invented situation leaves out morally or practically important facts, the audience may accept a conclusion that does not transfer fairly to the real case.
Study instruction:
- Create a hypothetical comparison that tests one principle, then identify which assumptions make the comparison work.
Key point:
- Hypothetical analogies use imagined cases to test or illuminate a claim.
- Their assumptions and limits must be made explicit.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
9. Q: Why are arguments from analogy generally inductive rather than deductively conclusive?
A: Show answer
An analogy usually provides evidence that a conclusion is plausible, not proof that it must be true. Even two cases with several important similarities may differ in a way that affects the target feature. The inference therefore depends on the strength, number, and relevance of the similarities, the absence of defeating differences, and the quality of the analogue evidence. A responsible arguer uses qualified language such as likely, plausible, or supported by the comparison when certainty is not justified. Treating an analogy as a deductive proof exaggerates what the reasoning can establish.
Study instruction:
- Replace absolute conclusions drawn from analogies with wording that reflects the actual degree of support.
Key point:
- Analogical reasoning normally supports probability rather than certainty.
- Confidence should match the strength of the comparison.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
10. Q: How can an analogy function as an explanation rather than an argument?
A: Show answer
An explanatory analogy makes an unfamiliar idea easier to understand by relating it to something more familiar. Its purpose may be to illuminate a process, relationship, or characteristic without asking the audience to accept a disputed conclusion. For example, a teacher may compare a complex system with a familiar mechanism to help learners form an initial mental model. The comparison should still be accurate enough not to mislead. An explanation becomes an argument when the comparison is used as a reason to accept a claim about the primary case, so the evaluator should first identify the communicative purpose.
Study instruction:
- Take one analogy and write two versions: one that explains a concept and one that uses the comparison to support a conclusion.
Key point:
- Explanatory analogies clarify; argumentative analogies provide reasons for a conclusion.
- Both require scrutiny, but the critical task differs.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
11. Q: What is the difference between an analogy and a metaphor?
A: Show answer
An analogy is usually an extended comparison that explains or supports a claim by identifying relevant relationships between two cases. A metaphor is generally a shorter, more literary comparison that draws attention to an aspect of experience and stimulates the audience's imagination. The forms can overlap: a metaphor may introduce an analogy, and an analogy may use figurative language. However, a metaphor's vividness does not establish an argument. When a comparison is offered as evidence, its relevant features and inferential link must withstand analysis rather than relying on poetic force alone.
Study instruction:
- Identify one metaphor and expand it into an explicit comparison, separating imagery from any conclusion it might imply.
Key point:
- Metaphors often emphasize expressive impact; analogies can make an explicit comparison-based case.
- Figurative force is not the same as evidential strength.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
12. Q: How can you determine whether a speaker intends an analogy to be an argument?
A: Show answer
Examine the surrounding context, the claim being advanced, and the role the comparison plays in the speaker's reasoning. If the comparison is offered as a reason to accept a judgment, policy, or conclusion about the primary case, it is functioning argumentatively. If it mainly makes an idea vivid or easier to understand, it may be explanatory or rhetorical. Some cases do both. Because intention is not always obvious, the evaluator should distinguish what the speaker clearly states from what the audience infers and avoid attributing a stronger argument than the speaker appears to intend.
Study instruction:
- Analyze a speech or article and mark the point where a comparison shifts from illustration to support for a conclusion.
Key point:
- Context determines whether the comparison is functioning as evidence.
- Do not assume every vivid analogy is an argument.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
13. Q: Why should the evaluator ask whether the claim about the analogue case is true or plausible?
A: Show answer
The first critical question is whether what is said about the analogue is actually true or at least plausible. An analogy cannot provide reliable support if its starting point is false, exaggerated, disputed, or selectively described. For a historical analogy, check the events and context. For a hypothetical analogy, examine whether the assumed facts and moral judgments are acceptable for the purpose of the comparison. If the analogue premise fails, the transfer to the primary case loses its foundation. This check should occur before debating how similar the two cases are.
Study instruction:
- Separate the factual claims about an analogue from the conclusion and identify what evidence would verify them.
Key point:
- A weak or false analogue premise undermines the whole comparison.
- Verify the comparison source before transferring its lesson.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
14. Q: How do you assess whether two cases share relevant similarities?
A: Show answer
Identify the features that are supposed to make the cases comparable, then ask whether those features bear on the target conclusion. Superficial resemblance, shared labels, or emotional associations may have little evidential value. Two situations can look alike while differing in the causal, legal, moral, or practical features that determine the outcome. A useful test is to explain why each similarity makes the target feature more likely in the primary case. If the explanation cannot be supplied, the similarity may be incidental rather than relevant.
Study instruction:
- For an analogy in a public debate, list the shared features and write one sentence explaining how each supports the conclusion.
Key point:
- Relevant similarities connect directly to the target feature.
- Surface resemblance is not enough to justify transfer.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
15. Q: Why must important dissimilarities between the primary and analogue cases be examined?
A: Show answer
Dissimilarities matter when they weaken the connection that the analogy relies on. A difference in scale, context, agency, available choices, causal mechanism, legal status, or consequences may make the target feature fail to transfer. Not every difference is relevant: two cases can differ in many incidental respects while remaining comparable in the feature under discussion. The task is to identify differences that affect the inference, not to reject every analogy because no two cases are identical. A strong evaluation explains how a particular dissimilarity changes the probability or meaning of the conclusion.
Study instruction:
- Identify three differences between an analogy's cases and decide which ones affect the target conclusion and why.
Key point:
- Only dissimilarities that undermine the inferential connection defeat the analogy.
- Perfect similarity is unnecessary, but relevant differences cannot be ignored.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
16. Q: What is a counteranalogy, and how does it test an argument from analogy?
A: Show answer
A counteranalogy offers a different comparison that challenges the original transfer of a feature or judgment. It may reveal that the original analogue was selectively chosen, that another comparison fits the primary case equally well, or that the conclusion depends on an assumption the audience has not examined. A counteranalogy does not automatically refute the first argument; it must itself be relevant and defensible. Its value is to expose the need for clearer criteria and to prevent an audience from treating one vivid comparison as the only reasonable way to understand the issue.
Study instruction:
- Construct a counteranalogy to a claim you encounter and explain which premise or similarity it challenges.
Key point:
- Counteranalogies test whether the original comparison is selective or incomplete.
- The countercomparison must also be evaluated critically.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
17. Q: How should the strength of a conclusion be judged in an argument from analogy?
A: Show answer
The conclusion should not claim more than the comparison can support. A limited analogy may justify asking a question, considering a possibility, or applying a cautious warning, but not necessarily adopting an identical legal rule or moral judgment. Evaluate the number and importance of relevant similarities, the severity of disanalogies, the quality of the analogue evidence, and the link between shared features and the target feature. If the argument moves from a narrow resemblance to a sweeping conclusion, it may overreach. The fifth critical question is therefore whether the conclusion is proportionate to the evidence.
Study instruction:
- Rewrite an overconfident analogical conclusion in a form that matches the evidence available.
Key point:
- Conclusion strength must be proportionate to the comparison.
- A plausible analogy may support a limited inference rather than a sweeping rule.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
18. Q: Why must the shared features be connected to the target feature?
A: Show answer
It is not enough to show that X and Y share several characteristics. The arguer must explain why those characteristics make the target feature Z likely to apply to X. For example, two public decisions may both involve personal sacrifice, but that resemblance alone does not establish that both decisions are morally required. The argument needs a defensible principle linking sacrifice, duty, public benefit, or another relevant factor to the conclusion. Without this bridge, the comparison is an observation rather than a complete argument. Identifying the bridge premise exposes hidden assumptions for evaluation.
Study instruction:
- For one analogy, write the missing sentence that explains why the shared features support the target conclusion.
Key point:
- Similarity must be inferentially relevant, not merely descriptive.
- The bridge between resemblance and conclusion is often an unstated premise.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
19. Q: What hidden assumptions should be exposed when reconstructing an analogy?
A: Show answer
Analogical arguments often rely on assumptions about which features matter, whether a precedent is representative, whether the same moral principle applies, and whether relevant circumstances are sufficiently alike. The arguer may also assume that the audience accepts the analogue's description or judgment. Reconstruction makes these assumptions visible so they can be tested rather than accepted silently. Ask what must be true for the comparison to support the conclusion, what evidence is missing, and whether another reasonable principle would produce a different result. Hidden assumptions are often where the argument is most vulnerable.
Study instruction:
- List at least two unstated assumptions in an analogy and determine what evidence or principle would justify each one.
Key point:
- Hidden premises often carry the most important part of the inference.
- Reconstruction turns implicit assumptions into testable claims.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
20. Q: How can an argument from analogy be reconstructed fairly?
A: Show answer
Begin by identifying the primary case, analogue case, target feature, and conclusion. Then state the analogue's relevant characteristics, the primary case's corresponding characteristics, and the proposed link between them. Preserve the arguer's actual point rather than strengthening or weakening it for convenience. Add only premises that are reasonably implied by the context, and mark uncertain assumptions as uncertain. This disciplined reconstruction allows the evaluator to test the reasoning rather than react to the wording, tone, or emotional appeal of the comparison.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct one analogy in numbered premises and a conclusion before evaluating its strength.
Key point:
- Fair reconstruction clarifies the argument before criticism begins.
- Separate explicit premises from assumptions added during interpretation.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
21. Q: How does the principle of charity improve the evaluation of analogies?
A: Show answer
The principle of charity encourages the evaluator to interpret an argument in a reasonable form that reflects the speaker's likely purpose, rather than attacking a distorted version. With analogies, this means identifying the strongest relevant comparison the speaker could plausibly intend while remaining honest about ambiguity. Charity does not require accepting the conclusion or inventing support that is absent. It helps distinguish a genuine weakness in the argument from a misunderstanding of what the comparison was meant to show. Where intention remains uncertain, the evaluator should acknowledge that uncertainty instead of presenting an interpretation as fact.
Study instruction:
- Write a charitable reconstruction of an analogy, then identify which parts remain unsupported even in its strongest reasonable form.
Key point:
- Charity improves fairness without lowering standards of evidence.
- A fair interpretation can still reveal a weak inference.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
22. Q: What makes an analogy a false or weak analogy?
A: Show answer
An analogy is weak when the cases do not share the features needed to support the target conclusion, when important differences defeat the comparison, or when the analogue premise is inaccurate. It can also be weak when the conclusion is much broader than the evidence permits. The label false analogy should not be used merely because two cases differ; all analogies involve some differences. A proper critique identifies the specific missing similarity or relevant disanalogy and explains why it affects the inference. This turns a dismissive label into a reasoned evaluation.
Study instruction:
- Find a weak analogy and explain the exact feature that fails to transfer rather than simply calling it false.
Key point:
- A weak analogy has an identifiable inferential defect.
- Criticism should explain why the defect matters to the conclusion.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
23. Q: How should the comparison between refusing vaccination and drink-driving be evaluated?
A: Show answer
The comparison described in the chapter argues that both choices can expose other people to risk even when harm is not intended. Its moral force depends on whether the relevant similarities concern foreseeable risk, personal responsibility, and effects on others. An evaluator should also examine differences in the nature of the conduct, evidence about risk, legal context, available alternatives, and the degree of control involved. The analogy may be intended as a moral criticism rather than a claim that the two acts should receive identical legal treatment. Clarifying the intended conclusion is essential before deciding whether the comparison is fair.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct the moral claim separately from any proposed legal consequence, then test both similarities and dissimilarities.
Key point:
- An analogy can support a moral comparison without establishing identical legal treatment.
- Evaluate the precise conclusion rather than reacting to the provocative comparison.

24. Q: What does the Gone with the Wind comparison illustrate about arguments from analogy?

The chapter describes a newspaper letter that criticizes explicit scenes in a television soap opera by comparing it with Gone with the Wind, which conveyed romantic passion without comparable nudity. The implied argument is that a programme can develop a romantic storyline without explicit scenes because a well-known example managed to do so. The comparison is rhetorically clear, but its strength depends on whether the genres, audience expectations, production context, and storytelling aims are sufficiently alike. A successful example may demonstrate possibility without proving that every programme should follow the same artistic choice.
Study instruction:
- Identify what the film example demonstrates and what it does not establish about the television programme.
Key point:
- An example can show that an alternative is possible without proving it is obligatory.
- Genre and context may be relevant disanalogies.

Amin argues that he accepted a role he did not personally desire because he believed it served the people, and that Nicholas should similarly accept the medical role because the public would benefit. The primary case is Nicholas's decision; the analogue is Amin's decision to become president; the target feature is duty overriding personal preference. The analogy is persuasive in form, but its force depends on whether the roles, duties, public benefits, and moral circumstances are genuinely comparable.

- A clear analogy can still depend on contested assumptions about duty and public benefit.

The analogy applies a set of behavioral descriptions to corporate conduct and uses the comparison to argue that corporate structures can produce socially harmful behavior. The comparison can stimulate analysis, but a vivid label should not replace careful evidence about corporate governance and conduct.

28. Q: What is an allegory, and how can it communicate an argument through analogy?
A: Show answer
An allegory is a fictional story whose characters, events, or setting carry symbolic meanings that relate to real-world issues. It may indirectly advance one or more arguments by inviting the audience to compare the fictional situation with political, social, or ethical circumstances. The chapter gives The Crucible as an example in which the Salem witch trials represent McCarthyism, and Don't Look Up as a story that stands for responses to the climate crisis. Allegories can create distance from contentious topics, but the evaluator should still examine how well the symbolic mapping fits the real issue.

- Allegories use fictional narratives to illuminate real-world concerns.

29. Q: How does Judith Jarvis Thomson's famous violinist analogy function in ethical reasoning?
A: Show answer
Thomson's hypothetical case asks the reader to imagine being attached without consent to a famous violinist who depends on the person's bodily support for nine months. The analogy is used to examine whether a person may be morally or legally required to provide bodily support to another, and to challenge the idea that denying abortion in cases of rape is automatically justified. Its force depends on the reader's judgment about bodily autonomy, consent, responsibility, and the relevance of the imagined case to pregnancy. The analogy clarifies a principle, but critics can dispute whether the cases share the morally decisive features.

30. Q: What is the structure of William Paley's watchmaker argument?
A: Show answer
Paley compares the complexity and apparent purpose of a watch with the complexity and apparent purpose found in nature. Because a watch is understood to be designed and constructed by an intelligent maker, he argues that nature should likewise be understood as the product of intelligent design. The comparison draws on features such as organized parts and apparent accommodation to an end. Its central inferential question is whether those similarities are strong enough to support the conclusion about the origin of nature. The evaluator must also consider differences between human artifacts and living systems, as well as alternative explanations.
Study instruction:
- Reconstruct Paley's argument by listing the shared features, the target conclusion, and the assumptions connecting them.
Key point:
- The watch is the analogue and nature is the primary case.
- Similarity in apparent organization does not settle the question of origin.

31. Q: How does David Hume challenge the watchmaker analogy?
A: Show answer
Hume's critique accepts that a watch is not a random occurrence and that some similarities between a watch and nature may be recognized. He then questions whether the dissimilarities are too significant for the inference. Living organisms differ from human-made artifacts in their irregularities, structures, and processes, and people can observe machines being designed while lacking equivalent direct observation of the origin of nature. Hume's challenge is not simply that the cases differ; it is that the differences and limits of evidence weaken the transfer from artifact to universe. He also asks whether another analogy may fit nature better.
Study instruction:
- Organize Hume's critique under the chapter's questions about truth, similarity, dissimilarity, counteranalogy, and conclusion strength.
Key point:
- A strong critique identifies differences that affect the inference.
- The existence of some similarities does not eliminate alternative explanations.

32. Q: What counteranalogy does Hume offer against the watchmaker argument?
A: Show answer
Hume suggests that the world may resemble an animal or vegetable more than it resembles a watch or knitting loom. If the world is more like a living organism, then generation or vegetation may provide a more appropriate analogue for its cause than human manufacture. This counteranalogy challenges Paley's choice of comparison and shows that a different analogue can direct the audience toward a different conclusion. The counteranalogy does not itself prove an alternative account of the world's origin; it demonstrates that the watchmaker comparison is not the only possible way to frame the issue.
Study instruction:
- Explain how Hume's living-organism comparison challenges the choice of the watch as the analogue.
Key point:
- Alternative analogues can reveal that an original comparison is not uniquely compelling.
- A counteranalogy challenges an inference without automatically proving its own conclusion.

33. Q: Why might an argument from analogy draw too strong a conclusion from limited evidence?
A: Show answer
An analogy may establish a resemblance without establishing that the resemblance explains the whole case. The arguer may move from a few shared characteristics to a broad claim about origin, cause, obligation, or policy. Hume's criticism of Paley illustrates this problem: even if the watch and nature share some features, the comparison may not justify a firm conclusion about the origin of nature. The evaluator should ask whether the argument supports only a possibility, a plausible hypothesis, or a stronger conclusion. Where competing analogies remain available, certainty is especially difficult to justify.
Study instruction:
- Identify the strongest conclusion the premises actually support and compare it with the conclusion the arguer states.
Key point:
- Do not infer more than the similarities and evidence warrant.
- Unresolved alternatives should reduce confidence in a definitive conclusion.

- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
34. Q: Why are familiar analogies often persuasive to an audience?
A: Show answer
Familiar analogues help an audience approach an unfamiliar or complicated issue through knowledge and associations it already possesses. A recognizable event, practice, or story can make a comparison easier to understand and remember. Familiarity may also carry emotional significance, which can increase attention and engagement. This persuasive advantage is not proof of validity. An audience may accept an analogy because it feels familiar or because it activates strong prior beliefs, even when the shared features are not relevant. Critical evaluation therefore separates the audience's recognition of the analogue from the evidential quality of the argument.
Study instruction:
- Identify what an audience already knows or feels about an analogue and assess whether that familiarity helps understanding or biases judgment.
Key point:
- Familiarity can increase attention and persuasive force.
- Familiarity does not establish that the inference is sound.

35. Q: How does an analogy frame the way an audience understands an issue?
A: Show answer
Framing occurs when a comparison supplies a familiar structure through which an audience interprets a less familiar case. The chosen analogue highlights certain features, makes some questions seem important, and may push other features into the background. For example, comparing a policy dispute to a safety problem directs attention toward risk and protection, while comparing it to a freedom issue emphasizes choice and autonomy. Neither frame is neutral. The evaluator should ask what the analogy makes visible, what it conceals, and whether the selected frame represents the primary case fairly.
Study instruction:
- Reframe one public issue using two different analogues and compare which features each frame emphasizes or suppresses.
Key point:
- Analogies guide attention and organize interpretation.
- A useful frame should illuminate rather than conceal relevant facts.

36. Q: How can emotional resonance make an analogy more persuasive?
A: Show answer
An arguer may select an analogue that evokes strong feelings because the audience already cares about it. A memorable disaster, injustice, sporting activity, or familiar personal experience can make a comparison emotionally immediate. This can motivate people to engage with a difficult topic, but it can also activate prejudice, fear, anger, or group loyalty. The emotional connection may overshadow questions about factual accuracy and relevant similarity. A critical reader should acknowledge the emotional effect while returning to the premises, differences, and strength of the conclusion.
Study instruction:
- Identify the emotion an analogy is designed to evoke and assess whether that emotion is relevant to the evidence or merely persuasive pressure.
Key point:
- Emotional resonance can focus attention and influence judgment.
- Emotion should not replace examination of the comparison.

37. Q: How can an analogy be rhetorically powerful but logically weak?
A: Show answer
A comparison can be memorable, emotionally charged, and easy to understand while resting on irrelevant similarities or ignoring decisive differences. Its vividness may cause an audience to feel that the conclusion is obvious before the reasoning has been examined. A metaphorical label can also make a person or institution seem blameworthy without demonstrating the specific behavior being alleged. Logical strength requires a defensible connection between the shared features and the target conclusion. Rhetorical effectiveness concerns how well the comparison captures attention and shapes perception; it should not be confused with evidential adequacy.
Study instruction:
- Choose a persuasive analogy and evaluate it twice: first for rhetorical impact, then for the validity and relevance of its premises.
Key point:
- Persuasive force and logical strength are different qualities.
- A compelling image still requires evidence and critical testing.

38. Q: What qualities make a metaphor or analogy memorable without making it confusing?
A: Show answer
The chapter presents a useful balance: a comparison should be unfamiliar enough to attract attention but recognizable enough for the audience to grasp its meaning immediately. A completely familiar expression may add little insight, while an obscure image may distract the reader. The comparison should capture an affinity between the cases in language that is vivid but clear. This requires understanding the audience's knowledge and choosing an image that expresses the intended relationship without forcing the audience to decode unrelated details.
Study instruction:
- Revise a vague metaphor into a clear, distinctive comparison and ask a reader to explain what relationship it communicates.
Key point:
- Effective figurative language balances novelty with immediate recognition.
- Clarity is necessary for a comparison to illuminate rather than confuse.

39. Q: Why does the placement of an analogy or metaphor within a message matter?
A: Show answer
Placement affects how the audience organizes and remembers the message. A carefully chosen comparison introduced early can provide a frame for understanding later points, while an analogy placed after the audience has received necessary background may make a complex idea easier to grasp. Multiple competing metaphors can create clutter and weaken coherence. The chapter discusses the idea that a well-positioned metaphor can provide an organizing structure for the arguments that follow. The best position depends on the message's purpose and on whether the audience needs context before the comparison will make sense.
Study instruction:
- Take a short presentation and test whether its central analogy works better as an opening frame or after the background explanation.
Key point:
- Position influences comprehension and memory.
- A consistent central comparison can organize a message more effectively than many competing images.

The metaphor communicates parental vulnerability in a way many listeners can recognize immediately. It helps organize the speech's concern with protecting children while avoiding a series of unrelated images. The example shows how a carefully selected metaphor can connect an audience's lived experience to a broader argument. Its emotional power should still be understood in the context of the speech's full reasoning and purpose.

These images translate a complex economic argument into a vivid picture of danger and possible loss of control. They also align with a fear-based frame, encouraging the audience to focus on uncertainty and adverse consequences. The example illustrates how a metaphor can support a political message by making an abstract risk feel immediate. 42. Q: What is the superior organisation theory of metaphor discussed in the chapter?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes a view that metaphors can help audiences understand and remember arguments by supplying a structure that links ideas together more effectively than disconnected literal statements. A well-chosen metaphor can act as an organizing frame, allowing listeners to see how several points relate to one another. For this to work, the image must fit the message, be rich enough to illuminate its main elements, and remain consistent. If the metaphor is too narrow or is extended beyond its useful limits, it can distort the subject rather than clarify it.

43. Q: Why is imagination an important disposition for using arguments from analogy?
A: Show answer
Imagination helps a person notice meaningful relationships between cases, construct illuminating comparisons, and anticipate how an audience may understand them. It is also needed to generate counteranalogies when testing another person's reasoning. This makes analogical reasoning creative but demanding: the arguer must understand the context, select the right features, and avoid inventing similarities that do not matter. Imagination is therefore not a substitute for evidence. It expands the range of comparisons that can be considered, after which critical questions determine whether a proposed analogy is defensible.
Study instruction:
- Practice generating two different analogies for the same issue, then evaluate both using the five critical questions.
Key point:
- Imagination generates comparisons and countercomparisons.

44. Q: Why can counteranalogies be easier to create than original analogies during a disagreement?
A: Show answer
Once another person has supplied an analogy, the evaluator already has a structure to work with: the primary case, analogue, and target feature are visible. It is then possible to search for a different historical example or imagined situation that shares the primary case's relevant features but leads to a different judgment. The chapter illustrates this with comparisons involving the Kosovo intervention, Vietnam, and the Crusades in response to an analogy used to justify the Iraq war. The counterexamples challenge the selective choice of precedent, although each still needs careful evaluation.
Study instruction:
- Take a historical analogy in a debate and construct a counteranalogy that tests whether the original precedent was selectively chosen.

- An existing analogy gives a starting structure for counterargument.

45. Q: How can a hypothetical analogy help people discuss a contentious issue more thoughtfully?
A: Show answer
A hypothetical analogy can create distance from a familiar controversy and allow participants to examine a principle without immediately reacting to their own personal commitments. The violinist example, for instance, invites readers to consider bodily autonomy and consent in a scenario different from their everyday experience. This distance may reduce some emotional interference and stimulate imagination. It does not guarantee neutrality: the invented case may contain assumptions that favor one conclusion. A productive discussion therefore examines both the insight created by the scenario and the limits of its resemblance to the real issue.
Study instruction:
- Create a hypothetical case that isolates one principle in a contentious debate and identify assumptions that might bias the result.

46. Q: How can stories and analogies reduce the emotional heat of ethical or political debate?
A: Show answer
A story can shift attention from the immediate identities and emotions of participants toward a case that can be examined at some distance. The chapter draws on the idea that literature and hypothetical cases can help people consider principles without becoming immediately caught in personal anger, jealousy, or entrenched positions. This distance can make discussion more thoughtful and open up questions that direct debate might obscure. However, a story is not automatically neutral. Its characters, framing, and assumptions may guide the audience toward a particular judgment, so the symbolic and argumentative work must still be examined.

- Compare a direct debate with a story-based version of the same ethical problem and note what becomes easier or harder to evaluate.

- Narrative distance can encourage reflection and imaginative engagement.

47. Q: How should analogies be used in public policy debates?
A: Show answer
In policy debates, analogies can help explain unfamiliar proposals, compare precedents, and highlight possible risks. They should be treated as tools for generating questions rather than automatic substitutes for policy analysis. Evaluators should compare the legal setting, affected populations, scale, incentives, institutional capacity, evidence base, and likely consequences of the cases. A historical precedent may suggest what to investigate, but it cannot establish that the same outcome will occur in a different context. Policy conclusions should also be supported by relevant data, legal analysis, and an explanation of uncertainty beyond the analogy itself.
Study instruction:
- For a policy proposal, identify the precedent used and list contextual factors that must be checked before transferring its lesson.
Key point:
- Analogies can guide inquiry but should not replace evidence about the actual policy.
- Context and consequences determine whether a precedent is relevant.

48. Q: How can you construct a responsible argument from analogy?
A: Show answer
Begin with a clearly defined primary case and a well-understood analogue. Identify the target feature and select similarities that are directly relevant to it. Verify the analogue facts, explain the principle connecting the similarities to the target feature, and disclose differences that may limit the inference. Consider at least one counteranalogy and adjust the conclusion to reflect the remaining uncertainty. A responsible analogy does not hide inconvenient facts or use emotional resemblance as a substitute for reasoning. It invites the audience to test the comparison rather than merely react to it.
Study instruction:
- Draft an analogy with explicit premises, a stated target feature, one relevant disanalogy, a counteranalogy, and a qualified conclusion.
Key point:
- Responsible analogies make their assumptions and limits visible.
- A qualified conclusion is stronger than an exaggerated one.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
49. Q: What common mistakes should be avoided when evaluating or presenting analogies?
A: Show answer
Common mistakes include choosing a comparison for emotional impact rather than relevance, treating superficial resemblance as decisive, ignoring disanalogies, misdescribing the analogue, failing to identify the target feature, and drawing a conclusion that is too strong. Another error is assuming that a counteranalogy automatically refutes the original argument, even when the counterexample is itself weak. Evaluators may also confuse an explanatory metaphor with evidence for a disputed claim. Avoid these errors by reconstructing the reasoning, checking the facts, examining both similarities and differences, and matching the conclusion to the support available.
Study instruction:
- Review a persuasive comparison and check it for factual error, irrelevant similarity, ignored difference, overclaiming, and weak counterargument.
Key point:
- Evaluate the reasoning rather than the emotional force of the image.
- Both the original analogy and its counteranalogy require scrutiny.
Source links:
- Chapter 9: Arguments from Analogy
50. Q: What is the chapter's integrated method for critically evaluating an argument from analogy?
A: Show answer
First, clarify whether the comparison is intended as an argument or an explanation. Identify the primary case, analogue case, target feature, and conclusion. Then ask five questions: is the analogue claim true or plausible; are the similarities relevant; are there differences that undermine them; can a convincing counteranalogy be developed; and is the conclusion proportionate to the comparison? Also examine audience framing, emotional resonance, and the assumptions that connect the cases. This method preserves the educational and persuasive value of analogy while preventing a vivid comparison from being mistaken for proof.
Study instruction:
- Apply the five critical questions to one historical and one hypothetical analogy, then write a balanced evaluation of each.
Key point:
- Test the analogue, similarities, dissimilarities, counteranalogies, and conclusion strength.
- Critical evaluation combines logical analysis with awareness of rhetorical influence.
